So, this video is really all about conditional probability, but we also have to learn a couple other things that conditional probability allows us to understand. So, a conditional probability is finding the probability of an event occurring, but taking into account that another event has already occurred. So, that's why it's called conditional probability. So we're find the probability of an event occurring on the condition that another event has already occurred. Now let's look at some examples of exactly what I'm talking about. So given that a girl has been selected, what is the probability she wears glasses? So I'm trying to find the probability that the person wears glasses, but there's a condition that it has to be a girl. That was given. That's the condition. Another example says, given that a coin lands on heads, what is the probability that green is spun? Okay. So, maybe I have a a spinner with different colors on it and I have a coin. So, I'm trying to find the probability that I spin green on the spinner, but there's a condition that I have to also flip a coin and get ahead because that's the given. That's the condition. What is the probability of selecting a man given that he is a Democrat? So, I'm trying to find the probability that I randomly select a man, but there's a condition. He has to be a Democrat. What is the probability of getting an orange Skittles given the Skittles is green? Okay, well the answer to that should be zero. But the idea is that we're trying to find the probability of getting an orange Skittles, but there's a condition that must come first and that the Skittles is green. And well, you can't have a green and an orange Skittle, which is why the probability there would be simple zero. Last example. If a smoker is examined, what is the probability he or she has lung cancer? So, I'm trying to find the probability the person has lung cancer, but there's a condition that they must have been a smoker. If you're not a smoker, well, I don't really care about you in this particular case because I'm looking at if you are a smoker, what's the probability of lung cancer? So, all of these questions deal with probabilities, but there's a condition, a given, something that was given to them first. So, the idea is that in each question, there is a given or an outcome that we already know to have occurred or be true. So, generically, this looks like this. What is the probability of A given B? So we're trying to find the probability of A but we're given that B has already occurred or another way we could say that is given B what is the probability of A? So we're still trying to find the probability of A but we're given that B has occurred. And one more way we could ask this what is the probability comma given B that A occur. So again we're still trying to find the probability that A occurs but we're given that B. So either way you say it and the English language allows us to actually say this three different ways B is the condition whether you put the given B in the back or you put the given B in the front or you put the given B in the middle it doesn't matter B is the condition and because it is known to have occurred now we want to find the probability of A. So the probability that event A will occur given that event B has occurred is called a conditional probability. And here's how we write it. So we write it with P for probability. Right? That first P right there is for probability, proportion or percentage. And in front right here is what we want to happen. That's the probability the event that we're trying to find the probability of. And then we have this line. This line basically is read given. And after the line is the given, the condition, right? So this is the condition right here. Now, English language allows us to put the condition at the beginning of the sentence, the end of the sentence, or even the middle of the sentence. But when we write it out using proper statistical notation, whatever we're trying to find the probability of of goes first, and then after the line is the condition or the event that has already been given to occur. All right, so that's it for conditional probabilities. But the issue is this. If we revisit the conditional formula, we actually kind of create another formula. Let me explain. So here is our conditional form that we already talked about. Now with a little bit of algebra instead of dividing by the probability of B I can multiply the probability of B to both sides and then I will get this statement. The probability of A and B that's this top part right here is equal to the probability of B multiplied by the probability of A given B. So what I just did there and again all I did was took this division of the probability of B and multiplied it to the other side and I just found a new formula for the probability of A and B. So to find the probability that event A and event B happens is the probability of B times the probability of A given that B has occurred. And here's the cool thing. I could even do this a different way. So notice I talked about earlier about I could switch these up. Now I'm trying to find the probability of B given that A has occurred and that formula is going to be on top still the probability of A and B but the bottom is the probability of the condition which is now A. But the same thing if I multiply the probability of a to both sides using a little bit of algebra I get a new formula. The probability of a and b equals the probability of a times the probability of b given a. Now why is this important? Because this leads me to something called the multiplication rule. Because notice, well, I'm multiplying. All right. So the multiplication rule says this. The general multiplication rule states that the probability that events A and B will occur. Right? That's what we're trying to find. The probability of A and B is equal to the probability that event A will occur multiply by the conditional probability that event B will occur given that A has occurred. Okay, that sounds pretty confusing in written form, but let's look at that formula I just built. If we're trying to find the probability of A and B, two events occurring at the same time, we simply have to take the probability of A, that's the first event, and multiply by the probability of B on the condition that A is occurred because guess what? A did occur first. So this is an awesome formula that's going to be very useful to you to find the probability of two things happening. This happens and then this happens. We take the probability of the first event A and then we multiply by the probability of the second event B, but we have to multiply by the conditional probability that A has already occurred. So let's look at an example of this really really quickly because I want to, you know, the the rule is kind of confusing, but I want you to see how easy it is to put into motion. A jar contains 20 marbles, 10 blue, four green, six red. You reach in and select a marble. Then without replacement, you reach in again and select another marble. What is the probability they're both green? So I'm trying to find the probability I get a green and a green, right? I'm trying to find the probability I get two green marbles, green and green. I want the first marble to be green and then the second. Okay, so let's use the general multiplication rule. All right, what's the probability that the very first marble I select is green? Well, there are four green marbles out of 20 total marbles. So, boom, there's my probability. But now I need to get another green marble. But now I have to think conditionally. I've already gotten one green marble and I did not replace it. Which means there are now three green marbles left in the jar out of 19 total marbles left in the jar. So this is a perfect example of me using that general multiplication rule. I found the probability that the first event, reaching into the jar and getting a green marble, occurs. And then I multiplied it by the conditional probability of getting another green marble on the condition that that first one was green. And all I had to do was use my brain to think about that. If I took that green marble out, there's only three green marbles left in the jar. And there's only 19 total marbles left in the jar. So this right here is conditional probability. This right here is a conditional probability that I didn't even need a fancy formula to figure out. I just thought about it, right? I just thought about the concept that there's 19 total marbles left and three of them are green. And that allowed me to quickly generate a conditional probability. And of course, you can multiply that out to get your final answer. So at the end of the day, if you are find the probability of multiple events occurring, you need to multiply them. I mean literally multiple things occur you multiply but you must consider how the outcome of the first event may impact the probability of the next event which is exactly what conditional probability is. All right, let's move on to another scenario that is somewhat similar, but a little different. A disease affects 1% of a population. A test correctly identifies someone with the disease 95% of the time. That's a true positive. You have the disease and it tests positive. It incorrectly indicates disease in 8% of healthy people. That's a false positive. So it says you have the disease, but you really don't. So let's let D being have the disease and T being test positive. Now again, we have to recognize that these are conditional probabilities. If you have the disease, there's a 95% chance of a positive. If you do not have the disease, there's an 8% chance of a positive. So let's use a tree diagram to really make this easy. All right, here is a person. Now, first the person can have the disease which good news very rare 1%. Or they could not have the disease and that would be very simple to calculate in our brains using the complement rule 99%. Now then we're going to take the test. If you have the disease, the probability of getting a positive result on the test is 95%. And then using the complement rule, we get 5% will get a negative result. Now that's called a false negative. meaning you have the disease but it says you don't. That sometimes happens in medical testing. Now if we do not have the disease which means we're healthy then we could still get a positive result 8% of the time but complement rule says 92% of the time we'll get the negative result which is what it should say. I don't if I'm healthy I don't have the disease should get a negative result but that only happens 92% of the time. So the important thing to understand is that these are conditional probabilities because for either of these probabilities to occur we have to first either have the disease or not have the disease. Now the ends of these branches would be again joint probability. This for example be the probability that we have the disease and we test positive. And but to get to that branch we got to multiply. And remember, one topic at a time, one step closer to a five. So let's start off with talking about independent events. It's half of what this topic is all about. So events A and B are independent if and only if knowing whether event A has occurred or will occur does not change the probability that event B will occur. So let's get this straight. Independent events means two events have nothing to do with each other. So one occurs, one doesn't occur, the other occurs, the other doesn't occur. They do not affect each other. So one event's occurrence has no impact on the other event. So again that's exactly what this is saying. Two events are considered independent if and only if knowing whether event A has occurred or will occur has no bearing does not change the probability that event B will occur. Okay, so basically the outcome event A has no bearing on the probability event B occurring or vice versa. So how do we actually show statistically that two events are truly independent events? Well, here we go. This is the way to do it. This is essentially a really really easy formula. All right. So if we could show that the probability of A given B equals the probability of A. If these two things are equal, then we are proving that they're independent. Now how does this formula do that? Well, on the right hand side here, we have the probability of A. Meaning the probability of A occurring. Does B occur? Does B not occur? I don't care. It's the probability of A. On the left hand side here we have the probability of A but it takes into account B occurring. So if B occurs and the probability of A is the same as the probability of A when we didn't even think about B. Well then that's proving that B had nothing to do with A which means that they are independent. I know that can be a little bit tricky and we're going to kind of go through several examples to make sure we explain that as best as possible. Now, here's the best part. We can also switch them around because saying A and B are independent is the same thing as saying B and A are independent. So, here we're looking at the probability of B. The probability of B. Now, did A occur? Did A not occur? I don't really care. I'm just looking at the probability of B. But on the left hand side, we're looking at the probability of B on the condition that A occurred. And if these two probabilities are equal, well then that just goes to show that A did not change the probability of B. Because on the right hand side, we basically don't care about A. But on the left hand side, we do care about A occurring. And if A occurring doesn't impact B, well then I guess we're independent. All right. Now, there is a second way that we could also prove that two events are independent. The idea is that it doesn't matter, right? Whatever you put here, whatever letter goes right here has to be the letter that goes right here. So if I'm trying to prove that A and B are independent. The probability of event D is 0.51. That's the condition goes in the denominator. Divide and we get.137. So given that D has occurred the probability of E occurring is.137 or 13.7%. All right. And we just calculated the probability of E given D is.137 and those are certainly not equal. So sorry, they are not independent which means that they have an association. So something about event D occurring made E less likely, which means D had an impact on E. Well, that means you're not independent. All right, hopefully that made a lot of sense. So, this topic starts off with random variables. So, we should probably start off with learning about what a random variable is. Now, a random variable is a variable whose values have a numerical outcome that results from a random process. So, obviously we know what the word variable is, right? You probably learned that back in algebra class. A variable is a letter that, well, you don't know what number it represents. That's very similar to what a random variable is. It's it's a variable whose values have numerical outcome. So very very similar to a variable in algebra but the idea is that the actual outcome the number that we don't know the variable is comes from the result of a random process. So if a random process has multiple outcomes and those outcomes are numbers then we have what's called a random variable. Now we use capital letters to represent a random variable. So capital A, capital B, capital X, you get the idea. Okay, we try to stay away from like O because it looks like a zero. You get the point. Now, here's some examples. So, if we roll a dieice, we're going to get 1 2 3 4 5 or six. Those are numbers. So, we could let the random variable X represent the outcome of rolling a dieice. Again, the outcomes of the random process of rolling a dieice are numbers. And those numbers are 1 2 3 4 5 six. So, that is why this is a random variable. We don't know before we roll that die what the outcome is going to be. But we do know that it's a random number 1 2 3 4 5 or six. So that's why we call it a random variable. And let's let the W, the capital letter W, represent the number of races a horse wins in two races. So if a horse enters two races, the horse could win both of them, that's two. Or it can only win one, that's one. Or it could win none, which would be zero. So again, the outcome of this concept, the outcome of this random variable is either going to be zero wins, one win, or two wins. Hence, that's a random variable because the outcomes are numbers 0, 1, or two. Let's let the random variable t represent the number of seconds to brush your teeth. Okay, you go to brush your teeth, how many seconds does it take? 82 seconds. Okay, well, that's a number, right? So, the outcome of this random process, because every time I go and brush my teeth, it's not going to be exactly 81 seconds every single time. It's well, random. Sometimes it's less, sometimes it's more, but the outcome is a number. And that's why it's a random variable. And the final example is H. The height in inches of a randomly selected man. If you go and grab a man off the street, he could be 71 in tall, 62 in tall, 74 in tall, 68 in tall. And you can actually even have decimals. So, you know, 70.34 in tall. The idea is that it's random. What guy I grab off the street is a random. That's a random process, right? I don't know what his height's going to be, but the result is going to be a number. So, as long as the answer is a number, you got yourself a random variable. Now, what do we call a random process that has outcomes that are words? Like when you toss a coin, heads or tails. Those are not numbers. Those are words. Well, we just call them regular old random processes. So, we could only use this super cool the super cool term random variable if the outcome is going to be a number. Okay. Now here's the next big thing. There are two types of random variables that we are going to be working with in this unit. The first type is a discrete. The second type is called continuous. So we kind of need to know the difference. So a discrete random variable is the outcomes. Remember random variables, the outcomes are numbers. The outcomes are listable and finite. So here we're typically thinking whole numbers because you could list whole numbers. 1 2 3 4 5 six. And when I say listable, I means that like you could literally make a list like rolling a dieice. I could list the outcomes 1, two, three, four, five, six. That is a discrete random variable. Again, outcomes are listable and finite. We know exactly how many there are. And typically, we're thinking whole numbers. Now, what about a continuous? A continuous random variable. The outcomes are infinite, hence literally unlistable. They fall on an interval where any value is possible in that interval. So, for example, if we think about how fast an airplane can go, well, I guess an airplane can go anywhere from zero to maybe 550 miles per hour. I don't know. I'm just spitball. I'm sure there's something that could go faster. But the idea is that when you think about decimals, any number in that interval is possible. Like an airplane can go 123.26 miles hour or it could go 123.27 miles per hour. And you start to be like, "Wow, I can't really list all of those outcomes, especially when you include decimals." So discrete, we're thinking, okay, I can actually list all the outcomes, and they're typically going to be whole numbers. Continuous. Wow, I got decimals galore. I can't list all these outcomes. There's just too many. All right, let's look at some examples of discrete so we can better understand this. Rolling a die. 1 2 3 4 5 six. Look, whole numbers. I just made a list. Hey stat stars, welcome to AP Statistics. In this video, we'll be covering topic 2.10 over the binomial distribution. If you haven't already, be sure to download the guided notes that accompany this lesson from the ultimate review packet for AP Statistics. There's a link in the description below. The guided notes will help you follow along and build a complete set of notes for the course. In this video, we will cover what the binomial distribution is and how once you know it, it will make calculating some tough probabilities pretty easy. As always, we'll begin by learning the key concepts and skills for this topic, and then we'll finish with AP style practice problems to help you apply what we've learned. Let's get started. And always remember, one topic at a time, one step closer to a five. So, before we dive too deep into the binomial distribution, we first have to understand what a binomial random variable is. A binomial random variable is a very specific type of discrete random variable. So, let's quickly remind ourselves what a discrete random variable is. So a random variable is the result of a random process is a numerical value. So it starts off with what we're going to be looking at today is random process meaning we don't know what the outcome is but the outcomes are listable numerical values. Discrete means listable and random variable means numerical values. So don't get overly confused this binomial random variable is this crazy brand new thing. It's really not. It's something that we've been working with a lot. It's just a very special type that we're going to talk about exactly how it becomes special in this video. But at the end of the day, it's simply a random process where the outcome is a listable numerical value. Now, here's the official definition of a binomial random variable. It's a little bit confusing, but let me read it anyway. A binomial random variable X, remember we typically use capital letters to represent random variable, is a discrete random variable that counts the number of successes in repeated independent trials that have only two possible outcomes, success or failure with the probability of success P and the probability of failure being one minus P. So the idea is that a binomial random variable, the outcomes are the number of successes that you could have in a set number of n trials. N just being a placeholder for how many. So for example, let's just say that Doug has a 15% chance of hitting a bullseye with a dart. And we know automatically that if he has a 15% chance of success that's hitting the bullseye, then the complement would be the 85% which would be one minus P. And that is where we get failure. And if he shoots, let's say five bullse eyes or excuse me, five darts, he's going to shoot five darts. The random variable, the binomial random variable is how many successes he has, how many times he will successfully hit the bullseye. So, if he has a set number of darts, five darts, he could hit all five of them. That'd be awesome, right? He could hit four of them. He could hit three of them. He could hit two of them. He could hit one of them, or oh my gosh, he could hit zero of them. He can't hit a negative amount. That doesn't make sense. He can't hit more than five because he's only shooting five darts. And he can't hit a decimal amount because how do you have 3.6 bullse eyes? That doesn't make sense. So again, this is discrete random variable. The outcomes are listable numerical values and those outcomes represent the number of successes that he could have in n trials and every trial the probability of success is 15% and then failure would be the opposite of that 85%. Now the other key thing here is we have to have what are called independent trials which means that what happens on one trial doesn't change the next. It's not like there's any conditional probabilities going on here where you say, well, if he hits the first two bullse eyes, then the probability of hitting him the third bullseye will go up to 90%. No, none of that happens. The probability of success stays the same. So, that is a binomial random variable described pretty quickly. Now, let's break it down a little bit deeper. There is a way that we could make sure that we have a binomial random variable. And there's four things that we have to check. And all four of these things are in that definition, but we're just going to break it down individually. The first is that every trial has to be what we call binary, meaning that it could either be a successful trial or a fail. Only two things can happen on every trial. What we want to happen or it doesn't happen. So that's it. It's got to be binary. That's why it's called a binomial random variable. The second thing is that those trials have to be independent. There could be no conditional probabilities here. Well, if this happens, then that happens. But if this happens, then this goes down. No, none of that. It has to be independent trials, which means what happens on one trial, success or fail has no bearing on the next changing or being uh higher or lower. And then the third thing is we have to be given a set number of trials, right? Because we need to figure out how many successes we could have in n trials. So n the number of trials must be given to us because otherwise it would just go on forever and ever. And the fourth and final thing is that the probability of success whatever it is given to you in the problem we call that P. Well, it has to stay the same. It cannot change from trial to trial to trial. And that means the probability of failure which is the opposite or the complement of the probability of success must also stay the same. Now one way we could remember this is using an acronym. We could remember B for binary, I for independent, T for number of trials and S for probability stays the same. So if we remember the acronym bits Bit Ts, we can remember the four things that we have to check to make sure we have a binomial random variable. All right, let's look at a couple of examples. This first one says McKenzie makes 78% of her free throws. If she shoots eight free throws, let the random variable M be how many she makes. So the question we have to answer is the random variable M a binomial random variable? Well, let's check our bits first. Is it binary? every shot she's either going to make the free throw or she's going to miss it. It's either going to be success it goes in or failure it does not. That's definitely binary. Independent means that there's no conditional probabilities. It's 78% every single time. It's not like if she makes the first, the probability goes up and if she misses the first, the probability goes down. So, we have independent trials. And then t is a set number of trials must be given. In this case, we were told we have eight trials. So, we're going to either make all eight free throws, seven, six, five, four, three, two, one, or no free throws. Those are the only outcomes for this discrete random variable. And then lastly, we have S. The probability must stay the same. So, we can't have any assumption like there's no hot streaks like, oh, McKenzie made four in a row. Now, the probability is going to change to 80% or 85%. Nope. The probability of success must stay the same. So, this would be considered a binomial random variable. How about the second one? Kayla makes 80% of her free throws, but if she makes any one free throw, the probability the next one is a make increases by 2%. So if she makes the first, then the next one's 82%, then she makes that one, then it goes 84%. But if she misses a free throw, it drops down 10%. She shoots 10 free throws, let the random variable K be how many she makes. All right, is this binomial? Well, first let's check our bits. Binomial? Yes, it either goes in or it goes out. independent. Do we have independent trials? And that is where we fail because we have some conditional probabilities. If she makes a free throw, it goes up 2%. If she misses a free throw, it goes down 10%. That violates independence. That means what happens on any one trial impacts the next and that violates the rules for binomial random variable. This is still a random variable, but it's not going to be a binomial random variable. So, sorry, this doesn't meet the requirements. Also failing is the S that stays the same. The probability certainly does not stay the same. We're literally telling you that it's going to change. All right, let's look at a third sample or a third example. In a small bag of 20 candies, 25% are red. Okay, 20 candies, 25% are red means that there are five red candies. Five out of 20 is 25%. If five candies were selected without replacement, let R be the random variable for how many candies are red. Okay, let's check our bit our bits. Is it binary? Yes, the cand is either red or it's not red. So, that checks out. Is it independent? Does the probability stay the same? And does each trial not have to do with the next? Well, this is where we run into a problem again. It says we're selecting without replacement. So, that means the first time I reach into the bag, there are five out of 20 red, which is 25%. Now, the next time I reach in, the probability is changing, right? Because if I got a red one, well, now there's only four red left out of 19 that are left in total because I did not replace it. So, 4 / 19 is 21 1%. So, the probability clearly changed. Now, it's 21.1%. And then, even if I did not get a red, well, that means there's still five red ones left out of 19. So again, the probability is still changing even if I did not get a red one. So five out of 19 is still 26.3%. So now the probability went up to 26.3%. So we also violate the probabilities are not staying the same and that means they're not independent. So what happens on this very first selection could impact the next or the next and so on and so on. So again, this is not a binomial random variable. Now, this next example is going to be very similar with a little bit of twist that will make it binomial. So, just hear me out on this one. At the candy factory, there's a batch of millions of candies. 25% are red. Okay, so 25% of millions. Well, I don't even know what that'd be because it doesn't tell me the exact number, but it would be a lot, right? Okay. Well, there are her eight shots, and we need all of them to be failures. Every single one of them is a failure. fail, fail, fail, fail, fail, fail, fail, fail. You get the idea. Now that we understand the process, let's look at a generic formula. Here is the formula for binomial probability. This first part is the number of combinations there are. Again, I call them combos. How many different combos are there? Now, remember, you don't have to know that crazy formula. The calculator will do it for you, but N is the set number of trials you have. X is the number of successes you're looking for. Then you have your successes. P is the probability of success. It's going to be raised to X, the number of successes you want to happen. Then you have your failures. One minus P is the probability of failure. And that's going to be raised to the N minus X. Now, where do we get N minus X from? Well, for example, if we have eight trials and we want to make three, well, eight minus three means there would be five failures. So, if you do n minus x, that should tell you how many failures you have. Most kids are just going to figure that out in their head. But that is the formula for the binomial function. This is going to calculate the probability of each individual outcome in a binomial formula. Now, here's the best part. This formula is awesome. I love it. All right, but there's one more thing I want to teach you before we dive into some examples. Let's now talk about the parameters of a binomial random uh binomial random variable. Now remember the pi the parameters are the mean and the standard deviation if the random variable were to be repeated a large number of times. So if we were to repeat repeat repeat this scenario, the average or the mean or the expected value is what we would expect in the long run. And this is a really really easy formula. N* P. Think about McKenzie for a second. if she were to shoot eight free throws over and over again, shoot eight, shoot eight, shoot eight, shoot eight. And maybe she did it multiple times day after day after day after day and she kept a journal and every day she wrote how many she made of those eight. N* P is just going to be the average in the long run. It's going to be the average of all those trials. So if we have eight shots and the probability of success is 78, 8 time 78 means that on average we would expect her to make 6.24 free throws. Now, in any one trial of shooting eight free throws, she's never going to make 6.24. It's impossible. She can make six, she can make seven. But listen, it's the average. It's what we expect to happen in the long run. So, if you know, before McKenzie steps up and shoots eight free throws, I would expect her to shoot 6.24 um makes in the long run. Okay, pretty simple. Then we have the standard deviation. Well, because guess what? This 6.24 is going to vary. Some days she might make more out of eight. Some days she might make less. So the formula here is n * p * 1 minus p. So what we're going to do in our square root here is we're going to do 8 * 78 *22 the probability of failure. So don't forget that square root 8 * 78 *22 is 1.17. So yes, we expect her to make 6.24 free throws, but sometimes she could make a little bit more, she could make a little bit less. So make sure you understand these, but be very careful. These formulas right here only work if you are a binomial random variable. They don't work for any other discrete random variable. In this video, we will cover what the normal distribution is and how it will make calculating probabilities for continuous random variables so easy. And it's hands down the most used probability distribution in the world. So get ready. And remember, one topic at a time, one step closer to a five. So we are going to dive into the normal distribution. Perhaps one of the most important topics of all of AP statistics in my opinion. But it all starts with us talking about what the heck a continuous random variable is. A continuous random variable is a variable that can take on any value within a specified domain. So remember, a random variable is the outcome of a random process is a numerical value. A continuous random variable means that that value, the numerical value I'm talking about, could take on literally any value with decimals included inside of a specified domain. So every interval within the domain has a probability associated with it. So when we talk about that a continuous random variable, there's lots of different possibilities for that random variable and every interval of possibilities has a probability associated with it. Now let's look at an example because I think an example is going to help this make a lot more sense. So let B equal the amount of time in seconds an adult brushes their teeth in the morning. This is definitely continuous random variable. I mean how long it could take you to brush your teeth in seconds, especially if you've got a good stopwatch. boy, it could be like 10.35 seconds, 10.36 seconds if you brush your teeth real short or it could be 199.6 seconds or 205.7 seconds. This is definitely continuous random variable. So for example, some particular intervals we could look at because remember there's a probability associated with every interval of a continuous random variable. So here we're looking at the probability. So P for probability that the amount of time it takes an adult to brush their teeth is less than 100 seconds. Or here's another one. We're looking for the probability that the amount of time it takes you to brush your teeth is somewhere between 60 and 120 seconds. And one more. What's the probability that the amount of time it takes you to brush your teeth is greater than 150 seconds? See, each one of these is an interval of values for that random variable. And every one of these has a probability associated with it. Now, the one thing we cannot do with a continuous random variable is calculate the exact value of one specific outcome. So if we said, hey, what's the probability that it will take you exactly 100 seconds to brush your teeth? Unfortunately, we cannot calculate that. When it comes to continuous random variables, we can only find probabilities with associated intervals of that continuous random variable. Now, how do we find these probabilities? Well, we're going to need to look at a probability distribution. So, here's a probability distribution for the amount of time B in seconds it takes an adult to brush her teeth in the morning. Now what we notice is a skewed right distribution meaning most adults take somewhere between 60 70 80 90 seconds to brush your teeth. It is very rare very unrealistic just very unlikely for it to take 130 150 160 seconds to brush your teeth. So the higher we go the more unlikely it is or the lower the probability is. So we see this with a beautiful curve. We actually call this a density curve. It's a curve that models the probability. underneath the curve is 100% or all of the possibilities. But then we could look at intervals. So example, we see that from 70 to 80 where it's quite high. That's where it's going to be more likely because it's higher. From 150 to 160, it's a lot lower. That's going to be less likely. So for example, one of our questions was, hey, what's the probability that the amount of time it takes to brush your teeth is less than 100 seconds. So what we do is we put a line at 100 seconds and then we look at the area underneath it. So the entire area underneath the the entire curve is 100%. So we just have to figure out what area is under 100 seconds that I highlighted here in blue. So I don't know, maybe you could say, you know, maybe that's about 65% of the entire area. You kind of have to eyeball it. Here's that second one we looked at. We said, what's the probability that the amount of time it takes an adult to brush your teeth is somewhere between 60 and 120 seconds? So we put a line at 60 line 120 and we look at that area in between and we try to figure out okay what fraction or what proportion of the overall area is in that shaded region and I don't know maybe 87% again I'm just spitballing but that is the answer. So if I decide that that area was about 87% of the whole area then that means about 87% of adults spent between 60 and 120 seconds to brush their teeth. And here is our final question. We said what about the probability that the amount of time is greater than 150 seconds. So we look at 150 seconds and we shade that very very very very very very very very small area above it. And then we say okay what proportion of the entire density curve what proportion of all the area under that curve is greater than 150. I would say very low maybe 5%. Again I'm just kind of making that up but again that's the idea. every particular interval for that continuous random variable does have a probability associated and we have to use a curve like this to figure that out. This is all awesome. We've been learning about continuous random variables. Hopefully, it all makes sense. But here's where it gets good. There is a particular curve that's called the normal distribution. And many continuous random variables are well modeled by a normal distribution. So this one right here that we saw earlier was what we saw a skewed right distribution. Again lots of things out there are skewed right where there's more data on the left less data on the right. Okay. But there's also a lot of data a lot a ton of data in fact that is considered normal. And that is exactly what this is. This weight of an adult male line is a great example of a normal distribution where we see a center peak where the majority of data is and then we see a a lowering on the left and a lowering on the right. Beautiful, nice and symmetric and lots of continuous random variables in the world we live in. Follow this normal distribution. All right, let's talk a little bit more about this normal distribution because it's really really cool and really really awesome to work with. All right, so for a couple here's a couple of examples of things that follow normal distributions. Heights of adult humans also weights and also animals too. When it comes to heights and weights of animals or humans, they often follow this normal distribution where the majority of people are in the middle around the mean, less and less to the right, less and less to the left. Scores on large standardized exams like the ACT scores, SAT score, and AP scores also follow a normal distribution where most kids are in the middle, fewer kids on the high side, and fewer kids on the low side. Blood pressure readings, manufacturing dimensions on m machinemade parts, contents of many food items, all follow normal distribution. Now you might contents of food items. Yeah, think about a box of Cheezits. How many ounces are in that box? Well, no two boxes are going to be identical, especially when you think about three, four, five decimal places. So what happens when we think about boxes of Cheezits? Most are going to be around a particular value in the middle. There's going to be very, very, very few really, really overfilled boxes and very, very, very, very few really underfilled boxes. Or think about a bag of Doritos, right? A bag of Doritos. There's no way they all weigh exactly the same. Yes, the bag might say, you know, 17 ounces, but there no ways exactly 17 ounces. That might be what we expect, but some are a little bit more and some are a little bit less. So, lots of things in the world we live in follow this beautiful, amazing normal distribution. Now, a normal distribution can be described as continuous. It does go forever both directions. Unimodal, which means we're going to see a center peak in the middle. Bell-shaped and symmetric. Now, we shake like a bell. You know, I don't know if everybody's familiar with this, but you know, think about bells around the holiday time. These normal curves are just very very famous. Like if you think of all the different distributions out there, the normal distribution is like the king. All right. So the normal distribution or the normal curve it's also called is in is identified by two parameters. The mean and the standard deviation. So to build one of these normal distributions, all you need to know is the mean and the standard deviation. That's it. Now, the mean is the center of the distribution. And this symbol we use for the mean is mu because we're talking about the mean of a population, not just the mean of a sample. And the value we would expect the random variable to be. That's the mean, right? It's going to be right in the center. It's like what do we expect that adult male line to be? Well, 450 pounds. That's what's right smack dab in the middle. Now, when it comes to standard deviation, the smaller the standard deviation, the taller and more concentrated the normal model is. The larger the standard deviation, the shorter and less concentrated the normal distribution is around the mean. So remember, standard deviation is a measurement of how far data is from the mean. So if we see this green curve here, the mean is going to be smack dab in the middle. That's where the majority of data falls, near the mean in the middle. But if we have a small standard deviation, most data is going to be near the mean, nice and compact, less spread out. If we have a larger standard deviation, that's what we're going to see here in red, where the mean is still in the middle, but it's more spread out because when your standard deviation is bigger, it means that more and more data is further and further and further from the mean. Here's another picture of this right here. We see three normal distributions. This one has a lower center. This one has a center right here. And this one has a center right here. So, this would be the mean for the first distribution, the mean for the second, and the mean for the third. Okay. Now, what about the spreads? Well, if you look at this third model right here, it has the smaller standard deviation because the bigger chunk of data is near the mean. It doesn't spread out to the left and to the right as much. And then the middle one right here would be this one right here. And then this one in the middle is going to have a kind of the bigger standard deviation. It has more data a little bit to the right, a little bit to the left. It's more spread out. So all standard normal distributions can be described based on knowing the mean and by knowing sigma, the standard deviation. All right. So let's look at a specific picture. So, this is where things get really, really awesome because we know that in a normal distribution, now again, you have to make sure that we know this is a normal distribution. The empirical rule I'm about to go over only applies if it's a normal distribution, approximately 68% of all observations are within one standard deviation of the mean. So, I've been saying that the majority of data is within one standard deviation, and it's well over the majority. It's 68%. 95% of observations fall within two standard deviations and 99.7% of observations are within three standard deviations of the mean. So the empirical rule is this idea of what's called the 68 95 99.7 rule. It tells us this when you follow a normal distribution and only when you follow a normal distribution we know that 68% of data falls within one standard deviation 95% falls within two standard deviation and 99.7% fall within three standard deviations. So that's exactly why we don't go above three or below negative three because it's so unlikely. Now it's not impossible because 99.7% is not 100% but it's pretty darn close. So again, it does give us that wiggle room that you know, Victor Rambayata, he is going to be up here somewhere with a very large Zcore because he is well above the mean. So he's definitely going to be outside of three because he's pretty special. Now, here's another view of this exact same thing, but it's just broken down. If we know that 68% of data is within one, we know there's 34% from the mean to one below, 34% from the mean to one above, all we're doing is taking that 68% and splitting it in half. Then we know there's 95% of data within two standard deviations. So from here to here is 95% of data. Now we already have 68% taken right here. So that means that the remaining 27% would get split up 13.5% between negative 1 and -2 13.5% between positive 1 and positive2. So in grand total there's the 95% right there. Now, honestly, don't worry about memorizing these numbers. It is a nice kind of simple way to do it if you can, but listen, we're going to learn how technology and our Desmos and calculus can do all this for us. And then 99.7% fall within three standard deviations. Now that means all the way from here all the way to here is 99.7%. Which means a whopping.3% of data again split up 0.15% here.15% here. So if 99.7% of data is within three standard deviations, 3% is outside of that but because of symmetry it gets split up 0.15% at the very very bottom and 0.15% for the very very top showing that Victor Bambayana is extremely extremely tall and that very very top echelon. All right. Now that's called the empirical world. It just helps us to remember how that data is broken down. 2.12
Hey statsars, welcome to AP statistics. In this video we'll be covering topic 2.12 over sampling distributions and the central limit theorem. If you haven't already, be sure to download the guided notes that accompany this lesson from the ultimate review packet for AP statistics. There's a link in the description below. The guided notes will help you follow along and build a complete set of notes for the course. In this video we will take our time learning exactly what a sampling distribution is and how they are vital to pretty much everything that has yet to come in AP statistics. As always, we'll begin by learning the key concepts and skills for this topic and we'll finish with AP style practice problems to help you apply what we've learned. Let's get started and remember, one topic at a time, one step closer to a five. So if we're going to start talking about what a sampling distribution is, well, we should probably start off with the definition. A sampling distribution of a statistic is the distribution of values of the statistic for all possible samples of a given size from a given population. Now let's be honest, if you're reading this or hearing this for the first time, you probably have no idea what that's talking about. Well, that's exactly what this video is going to cover, exactly what this definition means in a nice precise way that you could fully understand it. So let's just start off by talking about what a statistic is cuz we do see the word statistic pop up twice. A statistic is any numerical any numerical value that describes a sample. So if you have a sample of data and you have a number that's you're using to describe what you collected from that sample, that number is called a statistic. We have lots of different types of statistics. We got the proportion from a sample, a mean from a sample, a median from a sample, a range from a sample, and plenty others. But the two big ones that we focus on in this course more than anything else is the proportion from a sample. Like let's say we get a sample and it shows that 38% are boys. Or let's say we get a mean and we say, "Oh, we got a sample and the average height is 52 inches." Something like that lies. Those are the two most famous statistics that we work with, but we have learned plenty of other statistics. All right, let's dive into an example because I think the best way for you to understand a sampling distribution is to look at an example. So, let's look at an example with a sample proportion. Let's say that we know 27% of high school students in Texas own a bike. This means the population parameter is 0.27. That's not a P hat. That is the true population proportion. All right, this is again, this is the true one and only one value. Of all high school students in the entire state of Texas, we know that exactly 27% of them own a bike. Now, this is the one and true population parameter. But, here's where things get interesting. Now, imagine if a researcher were to select a random sample of 120 high school students from Texas and ask each if they own a bike. He or she would get what's called a sample proportion P hat. Now, we know the true population proportion is 27%, but that doesn't mean that a sample is going to match that exactly. This sample proportion would be considered a continuous random variable because before the researcher gets the final sample proportion value, the outcome is considered a random variable. And remember, what is a random variable? It is a numerical outcome of a chance process. So, when the researcher goes to get this sample of 120 high school students from the state of Texas, he or she has no idea what that sample proportion is going to be. Yes, the population proportion is 27%, but again, that doesn't mean the sample is going to be exactly 27% as well. So, that answer, the answer to the sample proportion is considered a random number because it's a number and we don't know what it is. Here are some examples. So, maybe we get 30 students that own a bike. That's 25% or maybe we get 31 students that own a bike and that's a little bit more than 25% or maybe we get 25 students that own a bike, and that's about 20.8%. The whole point is that there's lots of different options that could happen if you get a random group of 20 120 high school students and ask them if they own a bike or not, well, you might only get three. That's about 2.5%. Heck, you might get 91. That's 75.8%. But, the point is that there's lots of options. So, yes, the one true population proportion, the one true P, is 0.27. But, when we go and look at a sample, there are lots and lots and lots and lots and lots of possibilities for P hat, the sample proportion. So, the sampling distribution for the sample proportion would just show every single possible sample proportion possible from random samples taken from Texas high school students of sample size 120. And it also shows which values are more likely. So, again, let's go back to that definition of a sampling distribution. A sampling distribution shows you every possible value of a statistic. In this case, the sample proportion of students that own a bike in Texas from a sample of 120 Texas high school students. So, again, we know that when it comes to looking at sample proportions, there's lots and lots and lots and lots and lots and lots and lots can't say it enough different possibilities. A sampling distribution will show you every single one of those different possible sample proportions, and it will show you which ones are more likely. Now, you still might be a little bit confused, but again, it will start to come as we go through more and more of this video. Trust me. So, let's now look at a different example. This time we're going to look at example of sample means. Okay. Let's say the mean weight of all squirrels in the state of Pennsylvania is 565 g. This means that the population parameter mu, the true population mean, is 565 g. Again, this is the true mean of every single squirrel in the state of Pennsylvania. This is the one and only one true population parameter. But, now imagine if a researcher were to select a random sample of 40 squirrels from Pennsylvania and weigh each of them. Here we he or she could find the sample mean weight of the sample. Again, mu is the true population mean. That is one and only one value. But, if the researcher goes and gets a sample of 40 squirrels and looks at the mean of that sample, well, that's going to be an X bar and that number could vary. This sample mean could also be considered a continuous random variable. Again, because the before the researcher gets the final sample mean value, the outcome is a random number. Again, remember what a random number is. It's a numerical value that stems from a chance process. And before the researcher Now, again, the researcher knows the population mean is 565 g, but once that researcher gets that sample of 40 squirrels, he or she does not know what the sample mean is going to be and that's why it's considered a continuous random variable because it could be, well, lots of things. So, if the researcher gets a random sample of 40 squirrels, the mean weight could be 523 g, 325.7 g, 603.25 g, 125.75 g. I mean, there's just so many possibilities as to what that mean could be. Maybe it gets a couple heavier squirrels and it gets a higher value of 845 or even 1,025 or maybe it gets, you know, maybe some lower, smaller squirrels and it gets a mean of 485.1. The point is is yes, the population mean, the mean of all the squirrels in the state of Pennsylvania is 565 g. But, if we only look at a sample OF 40, WE'RE NOT guaranteed to get 565. We're going to get something different and that's going to be our sample mean, and well, sample means are going to vary. So, again, what does the sampling distribution show us? The sampling distribution for the sample mean would show every possible sample mean possible from random samples of size n equals 40 squirrels from Oh, that says Ohio. It should say Pennsylvania. Sorry about that typo. And it would show which values are more likely. So, again, remember our definition of a sampling distribution. A sampling distribution of a statistic is the distribution of all values of the sample statistic from all possible values of a given sample size. So, if the sample statistic that we're talking about is the proportion of kids in Texas that own a bike, well, then the sampling distribution would show all possible sample proportions of group of 120 students in Texas that own a bike. Or if that statistic is the sample mean weight of a sample of 40 squirrels, well, the sampling distribution would show every possible sample mean that's possible for a group of 40 squirrels. So, now the big question is, okay, now that we are slightly starting to understand what a sampling distribution is, how do we build one? Well, you start looking at samples. We take a sample of 120 Texas students, get a sample proportion, do another one, and another one, and another one, and then same thing with the squirrels. We get sample of 40 squirrels, a sample of 40 squirrels, a sample of 40 squirrels, and look at it the mean, look at the mean, look at the mean, and then we could start to look at all of them, and we could build a model that shows all of them, but that would take forever. So, the next thing we could do is simulate. The sampling distribution of a statistic can be simulated by repeatedly generating a large number of random samples from the population assuming known values for the parameter. The value of the statistic is determined and recorded for each sample. The resulting distribution of the sample statistic values approximate the sampling distribution. So, essentially what this is saying is to approximate what the sampling distribution going to look like. We can simulate a sample, record its sample statistic, simulate a sample, record its sample statistic, and eventually, after recording a bunch of possible sample statistics, we can get idea of what that's going to look like. So, for example, let's just say, again, it was true that 27% of kids in Texas, high school students in Texas, own their own bike. What we could do is simulate getting out a sample and of 120, and we would say, "Oh my gosh, this sample was 41%." So, we put a dot at 41%. Oh, the next sample was 20%. We put a dot at 20%. Oh, the next sample was 30%. We get a dot at 30%. Oh, the next sample was also 30%. We put another dot there. And every single dot would represent the sample proportion. And maybe we get a weird sample that shows 70% or another weird sample that only shows 2%, but if we look at more and more and more and more and more and more samples, we would get more and more and more and more and more sample proportions, and they would start to stack up on each other just like this, and we'd say, "Oh my gosh, notice that most of them are stacking up around 27%." Well, that's what's true. If 27% is true, we would expect a sample proportion to be 27%. It would be weird to be really, really high or really, really low. Now, 30% would not be weird. 26%, 25%, 20%, 21%, those would not be weird cuz those are all near 27%, but most sample proportions would start to be near 27%. And what we start to build here is a picture of all possible sample proportions, which is called a sampling distribution. We can do the same thing for the squirrels. If we knew that the true mean was 565 g, and we say, "All right, let's start looking at samples of 40. Let's simulate looking at samples of 40 squirrels." Maybe we get a sample mean of 558, so we put a dot right there. Maybe we get another sample with 572. Another sample with 560. And then we start to put dots at all the different possible values. But guess what? If the true mean is 565, getting a sample way over here is possible, but very unlikely. Getting a sample way down here is possible, but pretty unlikely. Because guess what? The truth is 565. So, most sample means would start to stack up closer and closer to 565. Yeah, 572 is just a little bit above. That's not too weird. 570, 570, 570. Yeah, 558 is just a little bit lower, not too weird. But most samples would start to stack up around the truth. And this distribution, this picture that I'm making, is the beginning of a sampling distribution showing every possible statistic from the samples. So, again, every one of these dots represents a sample mean of 40 squirrels. And yes, some are going to be on the high side and some are going to be on the low side, but most should be in the middle near that 565. All right. So, the best part is this. The sampling distribution that shows all possible sample proportions or all possible sample means has a very familiar characteristic. If you noticed those last two pictures I drew, you might have noticed that it was approximately normal. So, here's the cool thing. All sampling distributions, there's a couple conditions that we're going to learn about in the future, but for right now, sampling distributions will be normal. Meaning most sample distributions or most samples are going to stack up around the population value. Very few on the low side, very few on the high side. Same thing with proportions. Most are going to stack up around the truth of 27%. Some a little bit lower, some a little bit higher, and it's going to follow that kind of normal distribution. So, here's what we're saying. Is if we look at 120 Texas students, we would expect the majority of samples to stack up around 27%. Maybe a little bit higher, maybe a little bit lower, but that's where we should see the large majority I'm going to shade this in. The large majority of samples. Samples on the real high end, 40%, 50%, those should be unlikely. Why? Because the truth is 27%. Samples on the low end, 5%, 10%, 11%, those should be very unlikely. Why? Because the truth is 27%. Most samples should be somewhat close to the truth. And again, same thing for the squirrels. Here would be a beautiful picture of this. We would expect the majority of samples to stack up around the truth of 565. Being a little bit on the higher side would not be weird. Being a little bit on the lower side would not be weird. But being really, you know, having a sample of 40 squirrels being like 800, that would be strange. Again, why? Because the average is 565. Or getting a sample way down here of maybe 125 lb or 125 g. That would be very weird because most squirrels weigh 565. To get a sample like this, you'd have to have a whole bunch of really, really small squirrels. But that's unlikely to happen. What's likely to happen is that you're going to get a bunch of average squirrels, and that's why you're going to get something near the mean. Hey stat stars, welcome to the unit one part A summary review video covering exploring one variable data. Now before we get started, I want to mention one quick thing. However, the good news is that the content itself has not changed.
