However, the good news is that the content itself hasn't changed. The concepts, vocabulary, definitions, statistical techniques, and problem-solving skills covered in this video are all still essential for success in AP Statistics. So, if you're looking for a comprehensive review of collecting data, you're in the right place. Everything you need to know is right here. So, sit back, grab your notes, and let's review the key concepts together. And don't forget to check out the ultimate review packet for AP Statistics. Inside, you'll find study guides, guided notes, practice worksheets, review videos, answer keys, and many other resources designed to help you prepare for your unit test throughout the year, and ultimately for success on the AP Stats exam in May. Let's get started. So, Unit 3 is all about collecting data, the good, the bad, the ugly, but mostly the good stuff. Here's a quick glance of all the different topics that are in Unit 3, but it essentially comes down to two big things: planning and conducting a study, and how to set up a good experiment. Those are the big topics that are kind of covered in every little tiny detail in this unit. Now, before you do any kind of data collection, it starts with one thing, asking a question. You need a research question before you can even start collecting data. Research questions can aim to learn about a categorical or quantitative variable for a population, or they can aim to look at the relationship between two variables. Couple of examples are, maybe you read that 62.5% of high school graduates go on to college, and you wonder, "What is that value for your particular state?" Or maybe you're wondering, "Hey, is the average amount of time a freshman spends on homework equal, greater than, or less than the average amount of time that a senior spends on homework?" Or maybe you love flowers, and you simply want to know, "Does a new fertilizer help make your flowers grow bigger, and taller, and more beautiful?" All of these types of questions can be answered through research studies. Now, to begin a research study, the very first thing you have to do is collect some data. But, that data is only going to be good if we trust it and feel that it's truthful. When we begin this process of collecting data, the first thing we have to consider is our population. That is the set of all possible subjects or individuals that we're interested in. For example, are we interested in graduates from New Jersey, or are we interested in high school graduates from California? Are we interested in growing a specific type of flower, or are we just talking about all flowers in general? So, the population really does matter. Now, sometimes, if your population's too big, you can't collect data from the entire population, so you just look at data from a sample, which is a subset taken from that population. For example, a group of seniors selected from New Jersey. Often times, our population is so big that we can't collect data from the entire population, so we collect data from a sample, which is a subset taken from that population. Our goal of a sample is to use the information from that sample to make generalizations to the larger population from which that sample came from. Now, once you have your research question, and you know your population, then you can start to identify how you're going to collect that data. There are essentially two types of studies: an observational study, and an experimental study. An observational study is when no treatments are imposed or given out to the subjects in the sample. They're simply analyzed, and tracked, and followed, and questions can be asked, but you're just observing. Now, this can be actually done in two ways. The first type is called a retrospective observational study. Here, we collect data from a sample of objects or people and we analyze that data. Maybe it's data that we collected in the past and it's data that has already occurred. Are we asking the subjects questions about things that have already happened? The second type of observational study is a prospective study. This is where we select our subjects, we select our sample, and then we track them and ask questions, and we observe them moving on into the future. A retrospective study could be something like selecting a group of freshmen, selecting a group of seniors, and then asking them, "Hey, how many minutes did you spend on homework over the last week?" Whereas a prospective study would be selecting a group of freshmen, selecting a group of seniors, and then saying, "All right, hey, for the next week, I want you to track or maybe we're going to actually fill or follow you to see how many minutes you are spending on homework in the future week coming ahead. Now, regardless of it's a retrospective or prospective observational study, the key idea here is that we are not imposing any treatments. We're not giving the subjects in the sample anything to do. We're simply Well, asking them questions, watching them, analyzing them, and often times we could ask questions through a sample survey, which might be just a verbal going back and forth asking questions, or maybe an actual survey on paper that asks them to fill some questions in. But, a sample survey is just a technique used through an observational observational study to collect data. A sample survey simply aims to collect data from a sample in hopes of learning what might be true for the larger population that that sample came from. Now, the second type of study is an experiment. In an experiment, different types of treatments are given or imposed upon the subjects or the participants to see how they're going to respond to those treatments. Now, in either observational or an experimental study, you could learn the relationship between two variables and how one might impact the other. But, only through an experiment can you actually establish what we call causation, meaning a cause-and-effect relationship. In an observational study, you're just observing, so you can learn some really cool information, some great relationships and associations between your two variables, but you can only establish a cause-and-effect relationship through an experiment where you are giving and imposing treatments onto your participants to see how they're going to respond. But, here's perhaps the most important key concept regardless of the type of study that you choose to do. Generalizations can only be made for a population if the data came from a random sample that is representative of the population that it came from. If it wasn't random and it's not representative, forget it, your data is not going to tell you the truth and not going to allow you to make generalizations to that larger population. Now, what are some of the key tasks that you might be asked to do on the AP exam in May that relate to what we're talking about right now. Well, first, they love to ask questions to make sure that you know the key details and the key differences between an observational study and experiment. Number one is that in an observational study, you just observe and watch. In an experiment, you actually give the subject something to do. And again, focusing on that causation, only with an experiment, not observational study, can you find a cause-and-effect relationship. The second thing that often comes out of this particular part of this unit for the AP exam is just reading a scenario and identifying, "Hey, that's an observational study versus that's an experiment." Hopefully, you have the tools to be able to do that. Now, when it comes to selecting a sample, there are five really good ways to do it that result in random and representative samples. Number one is a census, two a simple random sample, three a stratified random sample, four a cluster random sample, and five a systematic sample. In a census we collect data from the entire population of interest. Sounds awesome, right? Well, yeah, actually it is. But if your population is really, really big, then collecting data from the entire population is really, really, really difficult. But if your sample or excuse me, if your population is really, really small, like maybe you're the manager of a small company that only has 20 employees, then you can easily do a census. But again, they're really difficult when your populations are really big. A simple random sample is a sample collected where every single possible group of size n has the same chance of being selected. So, let's just say that you want to get a sample of 100 objects. Well, then any possible 100 objects from that population has the same chance of being selected. This is really easy to do. All you got to do is number every single individual object or person in your population, use what we call a random number generator, which just generates random numbers, ignore repeats, ignore numbers that nobody has, and then the corresponding people that get picked through those numbers from the random number generator are the ones that you pick for your sample. Stratified random sample first starts off to by dividing your population into groups that we call strata. Now, we create those groups based on some shared attributes that those individuals have. That means individuals within each strata are homogeneous, meaning they all have something in common. Once we have these stratas or these groups divided up, we then do a simple random sample taking some subjects from each and every group we create. So, let's just say we have five stratas and the people within each strata have a lot of shared common attributes. We then visit each group and we take a couple people from each group. How do we do that? Randomly, of course, through simple random sample. So, essentially we're doing five simple random samples in each of the strata to create our overall sample. A cluster random sample also involves breaking our population down into groups, but here we call them cluster. In each clusters will be called heterogeneous. Now, that means in each of the clusters we create, we have a mix of all the different attributes that are coming from our population. So, essentially each cluster is like a mini population because it's heterogeneous. It's got a nice mix. Then we don't do a simple random sample from each of the clusters. No, no, no. We do a simple random sample of the clusters. So, the clusters all get numbered and we select one or two, however many clusters you think is needed to become our entire sample. So, let's just say that we have 10 clusters. Each cluster is like a mini population, meaning it wouldn't matter which one gets picked because it will represent our population. So, maybe we label them one through 10 and then we randomly pick out two of them to become our sample. All individuals within each cluster picked creates my entire sample. Systematic random sampling is a method where we have a random starting point and from that random starting point, we have an interval of which we choose people by. Meaning maybe we have a random line of people and we have a random starting point and we select every 10th person in line or maybe we have a list of all of our individuals from our population. We have a random starting point and we select every 17th person from that list. That is where we have a systematic sample. Now, hearing all of these definitions and explanations of our sampling techniques is awesome, but you know what's truly best is to learn through an example. So, let's talk about these five different techniques with an actual example. So, let's just say a large manufacturer of crayons produces thousands and thousands of crayons every single day. Now, they do so over three shifts. You know, first shift, second shift, day shift, and they produce their crayons using five different machines. Our goal is to get a sample of crayons that we're going to analyze and maybe take a look at things like the weight of them, the length of them, the color of them, the how how you know, how dark or how light they color, all those different attributes that we could collect from crayons. Now, a census here would be literally using every single crayon from a day's production. Now, that's thousands of crayons. Now, the advantage is we're going to get very accurate data to use for our investigation. But, the disadvantages is that's thousands of crayons. That's going to take a very large amount of time to analyze every single one of them. And the other disadvantage is if we have to use the crayons and measure the crayons, then can we sell those crayons? Then all of a sudden we just wasted a bunch of crayons. A simple random sample would start by giving every single crayon a unique number, 00001 through thousands and thousands that there are. Then we'd use a random number generator to randomly pick, let's say, 100 crayons. And every one of those numbers that are picked would correspond to a specific crayon, and that crayon would become our sample. Got to make sure to ignore repeats cuz we can't have the same crayon picked twice, and ignore numbers that no crayon has. Now, this is going to be great. But, remember the key detail here. Any group of 100 has a chance to be picked. Now, the advantage here is it's really, well, simple. We're guaranteed to get 100 truly random crayons, and they should be representative. But, the disadvantage is this. Remember, any sample of 100 can be picked. Maybe we get all 100 blue crayons. Now, all of a sudden that's not representative. Now, a sample like that would be extremely unlikely, but there is simple random sample, it is possible. Or maybe all 100 crayons picked are from the second shift. And if the intention of our study is to compare the crayons made from the three different shifts, then all of a sudden only having crayons from the second shift is not going to help answer our research question. Or another example could be if all 100 crayons are from the fourth machine. And again, if the intention of our research question is to compare the machines, then all of a sudden only having 100 crayons from that fourth machine might not be the best idea. Now, all these weird different samples that you could come up with in your mind might be very, very unlikely, but the most important thing is that they are possible when you do a simple random sample. So, there's a lot of good advantages to a simple random sample because it's so simple, but at the same time if you truly need representation, you might not get it. So, we're seeing that there are advantages and disadvantages to all of these different methods. Now, how do we know which one's best? Well, you have to really take into account two things: your population and your research question. If my research question doesn't care about the machine that the crayon came from or that the shift it was made on or its color, then a simple random sample is going to be great. But if my research question is really focused on the machine or the shifts or the color, well, then a simple random sample might not be the best idea. Now, a stratified random sample would begin by first dividing up the population of crayons into groups that have a shared attribute. Something as simple as all the blue crayons, all the green crayons, all the gray crayons, all the red crayons, all the orange crayons, all the green crayons, you separate by groups. Once you have all of those crayons separated, then what you have to do is visit each of those groups or strata we call them. Then you would conduct a simple random sample by taking a couple crayons from every single group. That's the key detail there. Once you have your groups that are very homogeneous within, you then select some from each. Now, you don't have to take the same number from each. If you have way more blue crayons, then maybe you take more blue crayons. If you have way less white crayons, then go ahead and take less white crayons. But, the most important thing is that you have to take some from each group. And the advantage here is you're going to get a very representative sample because you'll have every color. Now, if your research question has nothing to do with color, but it's all about the quality from the three different shifts, well, then maybe you stratify by shifts. You put all the crayons from the first shift together, second shift together, third shift together, and then you do a simple random sample by taking, let's say, I don't know, 75 crayons from each of those three shifts. Again, that's now going to give you the representation of being able to analyze all three shifts because you know for a fact that your sample contains crayons from all three shifts. Or, again, another example is if you really care about how those five different machines are operating, you stratify based on the machine that the crayons came from, and you'd make sure that you take a sample from each of those groups. So, the huge advantage to stratifying is that, well, you are guaranteed the representation that your research question desires. A disadvantage is you're doing multiple simple random samples, so sometimes it can take quite a long time because you got to go to this group and then label label all the crayons, go to this group, label all the crayons, go to this group, and then you got to take a couple from every single group. Again, you're guaranteed the representation, but it's going to be very time-consuming. A cluster random sample would maybe be used if the crayons are already in boxes. And a best-case scenario would be if every single box contains different colors, crayons from different shifts, crayons from different machines. Then, all of a sudden, every box is like, well, it's all little mini population representing all of the different types of crayons, colors, shapes, machines. You get it. Then what we do is we don't label the individual crayons, we label the individual boxes. So maybe we have 200 boxes of crayons and we label them 001 through 200. We use a simple random generator to just generate a couple numbers for us and maybe we select five numbers and those five boxes and every crayon in those five boxes is used in our sample. But the key idea here is that we have these mini populations, those were our clusters, and we don't take some from each cluster, we just pick a couple of the clusters randomly of course to become our entire sample. Now, a great advantage to a cluster sample is it's much, much faster. You only have to label the boxes, you don't have to label all the individual crayons. Now, the disadvantage to a cluster sample is if you don't have that perfect scenario where every box is a good representation of what your research question is focusing on, then maybe it doesn't work. You get the idea, I hope. For example, maybe the boxes are by color. You got a box of blue, another box of red, another box of blue, a box of all yellows, two boxes of all orange, six boxes of all green, whatever. So if you look at those as your clusters, that's a terrible idea because then if you only pick two boxes, then maybe you get the two blue boxes and all of the sudden you're not representative. So you're going to be very careful about how you set up those clusters because you want the clusters to be again mini populations. That way no matter which one is picked, you're going to get that randomness and that representation. Now, a systematic random sample would be you take all the crayons, maybe you line them up and you had a random starting point and from that random starting point you select every 20th crayon until you have 100 total crayons. You just count one, two, three, four, five, oh, 20, 40, 60, and then every 20th crayon becomes selected for your sample. Now, what are some of the key questions that could be asked of you on the AP exam dealing with these different methods of selecting a sample? Number one is just reading a scenario and identifying it as a simple random sample, a census, stratified cluster, or systematic random sample. That's not too bad as long as you understand them. The second thing they like to focus it on is the advantages and the disadvantages of the different methods. They love to give you scenarios and make sure that you understand not only what scenario it is, but what's an advantage to doing it that way versus what's a disadvantage to doing it that way. Now, remember, I said this already, I'll say it one more time. When you're considering advantages and disadvantages, you have to consider two things: the population of interest and the research question. What is it focusing on and how will that dictate what type of method you want to use and why one might be better than another. Bias occurs when certain responses are systematically favored over others. This is really bad. Why? Well, think about it. If you are aiming to get the truth regardless of what it is, that's your goal, and some responses are systematically favored over others, then that means the data you collect is going to be leaning towards those responses that are favored, meaning that you're not going to get the truth that you want. And that's the problem with bias. Now, there's lots of different ways that you can have bias in your data. Let's examine some of them. Bias can come from how you select your sample or what you do with your sample. Meaning that maybe you have a great random sample, but what you do with it creates bias. Here are some of the different forms of bias: voluntary response bias, convenience bias, undercoverage bias, response bias, and nonresponse bias. Let's talk about these different types of bias through an example. Let's say our population of interest is high school students from the state of California, and our research question is focusing on the habits and opinions of illegal drug use from those students. And we have a sample survey for the students to fill out already to go. What are some things that could go wrong with how we collect a sample of high school students from the state of California? Well, one thing we could do that'd be really, really bad is asking for volunteers to fill out our sample survey about opinions and habits of illegal drug use. I mean, what type of student do you think is going to volunteer to fill out that survey? Probably kids that don't use drugs and have very low opinions of them. So, this is the problem you use volunteers. You're going to get strong opinions from either one side or the other. Then all of a sudden, you're not getting to that truth that you're really after. So, we pretty much always say, "Don't ever use volunteers. It's simply not random." A second thing you could do wrong when it comes to selecting your sample is using a convenience sample. This means anything that you do that's not random in terms of picking your students. So, maybe we just say, "Listen, I don't have a ton of time. I'm just going to drive to the closest high school in California, and I'm going to sample all the kids in that high school and give them my sample survey." Well, first and foremost, that's not random at all. That's a huge problem. And it might not be representative. I mean, maybe the high school that you visit is a very high socioeconomic with a lot of rich wealth in that district, and all of a sudden the opinions you get are not going to be really representative of the entire population. That's going to be a problem. So, don't ever pick a sample out of convenience for you. Imagine you have a great idea to do a cluster random sample, and you have clusters of all of the different high schools across the state of California. You envision that every high school is like a mini population. It's got boys, it's got girls, it's got this, it's got that. It's nice and random and nice and representative, right? Well, the problem here is if you go ahead and say, "Hey, listen, I'm going to number every single high school student of California, and I'm going to pick out five of them, and every student in those five schools will be my sample. What if the five schools that you just end up picking end up being private schools from really wealthy areas? Then all of a sudden the data you use for your research question is going to favor those types of students who might be like, "A-okay, we don't use drugs." And then all of a sudden you have a big, big problem. So, even though your intention was using a really good cluster sample, and you thought your clusters were mini populations, but if those different clusters, the different high schools, are very different from each other, then all of a sudden that's not a great idea. Remember, when you create clusters, you want any cluster being picked to be a great representation of the entire population, and that might not be true if you use the high schools as your mini population. In that scenario, you basically uncovered all other types of high schools if you only got five private high schools. Now, bias can also come from what you do after you have your sample. So, maybe you have the most perfect, random, representative sample from the students in the state of California that's ever, ever been created, all right? A-okay, this is great, this is awesome. But you say, "All right, your teachers are going to ask you the survey questions out loud in front of everybody else, do you use illegal drugs, what's your opinion on using drugs, what type of illegal drugs do you use?" Now, all of a sudden, because the teacher's asking the question, we have what's called response bias, because some students might not give a very truthful response because of the person asking the question. So, response bias is basically your response is untruthful for any reason. Another example of this could be if the wording of the question looks something like this. Now, another form of response bias could come in the way that you actually word your questions. Imagine you have this question. Illegal drug use causes hundreds of deaths every year and tons and tons of arrests. What do you think of illegal drug use? Well, if that's the way you word your question, you're probably going to get a lot of students that say, "Oh, I didn't I just think about all the people that are getting arrested and all the deaths that it's causing. Yeah, I'm against illegal drug use." Now, the problem there is that very first phrasing called leading wording or leading questions, right? The question was worded to lead you to a to a certain answer and that is of course going to cause response bias. So, how do we fix that? Just get rid of that leading words and ask the question straightforward and simply. Again, another example of what can happen after you select your sample is you again have the most perfect representative random sample ever, but 30% of the kids that are selected don't respond. We call this non-response bias. Now, what's the problem when people don't respond? Well, it's because maybe the reason they're not responding makes them very different than those that do respond. Then all of a sudden, you're only getting responses from those that do respond and if they're very different from the ones that don't respond, then you're going to be biased. You had all these people picked to be in your sample, you need to get the survey filled out by all of them. You can't allow some people to just not respond because again, maybe they're different and you want to know about those differences. Now, the big question is how do you fix these mistakes? Well, if the mistake happened in how you selected your sample, be smart, don't be dumb, be intelligent, plan correctly, think about your population, think about your research questions, and make sure you come up with a sampling technique that is going to truly produce a random and representative sample. Now, what about mistakes that happen after you have your sample? Well, in terms of response bias, make sure you do things like proofread and field test your questions to make sure they're going to give you good accurate results. Make sure that you have the option to have the survey be anonymous so kids are more likely to fill it out truthfully. And if people don't respond, you need to get their opinions and you need to get them to fill that survey. Maybe you call them, maybe you visit them, maybe you email them, maybe you bug them over and over again, or you can even incentivize them to fill out the survey. As long as that incentive doesn't, again, lead them to slighting certain opinions, it's fine. But you have to get their opinions. You can't just fix nonresponse bias by asking more people to fill in the gap. Not how it's going to work. Now, what are some of the key details here, the key things that the AP test can ask you about bias? Well, one, again, maybe multiple choice question, you have to read a couple scenarios and choose what type of bias are you seeing, whether it's response bias, nonresponse bias, undercoverage, all that kind of fun stuff. Hopefully, that's not too bad. The other thing they like to really focus in on is how do you fix the problem? So, maybe they'll give you a scenario where there's clearly an issue and they say, "Hey, how could you fix the issue? What can be done to make it better?" So, again, make sure you think about those things as well. Now, when it comes to experimental design, there are tons of vocabulary words that you need to know. And not only do you need to know them, but you need to understand them and be able to put them into use in a specific problem. So, here's a look at many of those vocab words. Let's actually take a look at an example to try to fully understand all of them. Researchers have a new medication to help memory in patients with Alzheimer's. Here's what they're going to do. They're going to get 100 patients with Alzheimer's, all different severities of it, to volunteer. Some are male, some are female, some are a little younger, some a little bit older. You get the idea. It's a nice mix of 100 patients with Alzheimer's. 50 of them are going to be given a new medication randomly and the other 50 are going to be given a placebo. Now, after 6 months of taking the medication that they were given, they're going to do a memory test again and the results showed a statistically significant improvement in those that took the new medication. Now, what does all this mean and what are all the key details that we could talk about with this example? First, this example may sound short and simple, but it really does contain all four of the most important principles that it comes to an experiment. All four of these principles have to be in your experiment if it's going to be deemed, well, good. We need to have comparison. We need to have random assignment of the treatments. We must have direct control to prevent confounding variables and we also need to have replication. The first vocab word here is experimental units. These are the subjects or individuals or people that are going to actually receive the treatments. For us, that is the 100 patients with Alzheimer's. The explanatory variable is the variable that we're going to manipulate. It's the It's the treatments, right? It's the variable that comes in different treatments. For us, that is the medication coming in two different treatments. That would be the new medication and the placebo. Now, why do we need the placebo? Well, a placebo is essentially a fake pill that does nothing. Why do we need that? Because of what's called the placebo effect. The placebo effect is this simple idea that when you're working with humans, it's human nature that when you are told you're getting something, that that simple knowledge of being told you're getting something might elicit a response. And the moment I know that I'm not getting anything, I'm all upset. I'm all sad. I didn't get anything and that can change my response as well. So, the only that we can control for the possible effect is through the use of a placebo. Basically, ensuring that everybody in our study goes home with something to take. That way, everybody experiences the placebo effect. Everybody thinks that they're taking something that's going to help them. Now, the idea here is that you can't get rid of the placebo effect. That's a something a lot of people think. They think, "Oh, I'm going to use a placebo to get rid of the placebo effect." No, it's actually opposite. You use a placebo to make sure that everybody has the placebo effect. Everybody thinks they're getting something. Everybody's excited to be a part of the experiment, and nobody goes home all upset and sad that they didn't get anything, and maybe that upset and sadness actually ruins their memory. Hopefully, you get the idea. So, you don't really need a placebo when you're working with objects or animals. When you're working with human beings, if you can use a placebo, please do so. The response variable is what you're going to measure at the end of your experiment. In our case, that is the difference in their memory tests on this um exam, whatever they're doing. So, we have the test results from before they took the medication, and the test results after, and we're hoping to see a difference. But, regardless, the response variable is what you're going to measure at the end. Now, let's talk about those four main principles. First, comparison. We definitely had comparison here because we had two different groups. One that took the new medication, and one that took a placebo. If everybody got the new medication, then we'd have nothing to compare results to to even know if taking the new medication was better because you can't say it's better if you don't have something to compare to. So, every experiment has to have at the very least two different treatment groups. Now, you could call the group that's taking the placebo, we call it a control group. A control group, by definition, is a group that gets nothing to use to compare to. You don't necessarily have to have a control group. Let's just say we were comparing our new medication to an old medication. Then, in that situation, we wouldn't necessarily need a group that gets the placebo because everybody's already taking something. But, if one group is literally getting nothing used to kind of compare your data to, that's going to be a control group. Now, the second principle we have here is random assignment of the treatments. What subjects get what treatment has got to be random. So, 100 people all get a number 001 through 100, use a random number generator, ignore repeats, ignore numbers that nobody has, and the first 50 numbers selected will correspond to those 50 patients who will receive the new medication, and the remaining 50 people will receive the placebo. Now, what is the true meaning of random assignment? Well, first off, it just sounds good to do it random, right? But, really more importantly, the real deep reason why we do random assignment is to evenly disperse all potential confounding variables that we just can't control for. Now, I mentioned things like gender, age, and severity of Alzheimer's amongst our patients. We would only hope that random assignment would sprinkle some of those different attributes into both groups. So, the idea through random assignment is that at the end we have these two groups that are very, very, very similar, right? This group is a nice mix of males, females, younger, older, severity high, mild severity of Alzheimer's, and guess what? Same with this group. So, through random assignment, we're crossing our fingers, and because it's random, right? We're going to get these two nice groups that are very, very, very similar between. The difference is what we give them. This group gets the placebo, this group gets the new medication. That's the idea. Random assignment helps us to actually control for confounding variables we cannot control for. The third pillar is control, direct control, for other potential confounding variables. Now, if there are confounding variables that you can control for, please do so. Remember, random assignment is to help for the ones that we actually can't control for. So, let's just say that everybody is going to take it for 6 months. That's a form of direct control. If one group took it for a year and one group took it for 6 months, then we have a huge confounding variable. Was it the time that related to this that caused patients to have better or worse memory, or was it the medication? And maybe something as simple as making sure that everybody takes it with a glass of water in the morning, because if you take it with milk in the afternoon, maybe that's why you have a better memory. It's the milk and taking it in the afternoon has nothing to do with the actual medication. I mean, just who knows? So, you got to control for the things that you can control for. That's something that's really important in every experiment. Everything else you can't control for, hey, you just leave up to random assignment. Now, the fourth and final pillar, or major principle, is replication. Replication is the idea that you want as many people in your study as possible. If we only had one person versus one person, new medication and old medication, or excuse me, new medication and placebo, well, then well, I mean, there's a million reasons why this person could have a better memory after 6 months than this person. That's not going to really help us determine if we have good valid results. But, if we have 50 people all taking the new medication and 50 people taking the placebo, and after 6 months the average memory increase of these people is significantly higher than these people, well, I think we're on to something. Now, you know what would be even better? Is if we had a thousand people taking the new medication and a thousand people taking the placebo. That would be even more replication. But, replication is the idea that having more and more subjects and experimental units within your study, within your experiment, is going to make it better and give you stronger, more valid results at the end. Some other terms that you may see here are what we call blinding. We have two different levels of blinding. We have single blinding. That is where the subjects involved in the experiment do not know what they're getting, which in our experiment we do have that. We do have the use of the placebo to make sure that everybody thinks they're taking something. The pills are all the same size, they're all the same color, that way there's no difference between them and nobody knows any better. Now, in some experiments, well, you really can't blind subjects. Maybe the treatments are things that you know the difference between and it is what it is, but if you can blind, certainly try to. Now, double blinding is when the research team that actually is involved in maybe giving the treatments or measuring the response variable at the end, they don't know what the subjects are getting either. That's something you should always think about doing as well. So, single blinding is when one group, the subjects or the research members are blinded, they don't know what's getting. Double blind is when both of those groups don't know what the subjects are getting. All this is just to prevent any type of bias from sinking into your experiment and ruining your results. Now, there are also three different ways to design your experiment. Basically, how do you want to set it up? The first is a completely randomized design, the second is a randomized block design, and the third is a matched pair design. The description I've already given you for our Alzheimer's patients example is a completely randomized design. We had 100 subjects and what 50 of them got and what the other 50 got was, well, completely random. In a completely randomized design, the goal is really, really simple. To allow randomness to create two or more very, very similar groups. You want these two groups to be very similar between, that way the only difference is what you give them. New medication, placebo. A randomized block design is used when you think there is a second, third, or maybe even fourth variable that you think could become confounding. So, for example, if I wanted to set our Alzheimer's patients example up as a block design, I would have to consider what other variables might matter in terms of increasing or decreasing memory. Now, one thing could be gender. Maybe gender, maybe maybe females just tend to have better memories as it is and males don't, who knows? The other thing that can definitely come into play is the severity of the Alzheimer's. If you are really severe, then you probably have a lot of really bad memory as it is. Or if it's very mild, maybe your memory isn't too bad yet. So, the idea here is that we might want to create blocks. So, a block would be a group of subjects that all have some shared common attributes, like all women with severe Alzheimer's. Another block could be all women with mild Alzheimer's. A third block could be all women, or excuse me, all men with severe Alzheimer's. And a fourth block could be all men with mild Alzheimer's. This way we're allowing that variable to be brought to the forefront and really pay attention to. Then, within each of those blocks, both treatments are given out randomly. So, in the women with severe Alzheimer's, half of them get the placebo, half of them get the new medication randomly. Same thing with the other blocks. So, in each block, both treatments are given out randomly. So, at the end of the day, we still got 50 people taking the new medication, we still got 50 people taking the placebo, but now we've blocked to really focus in, does gender matter? Does severity of Alzheimer's matter? If we just leave it up to completely random, then, you know, small chance of this happening, but maybe all the women get the new medication used medication and all the men get the placebo, then we have a huge confounding variable. Or maybe all the severe patients go into the new group and all the mild patients go into the placebo group. That creates a very large confounding variable of well, their memory got better because they were mild or their memory got worse cuz they were severe and we don't want that to happen. Again, you only have to worry about this if you're really concerned about another variable that could matter towards your response. Now, the third and final type of designs will be called a matched pair design. Here, we pair subjects up together. So, first, to perform a matched pair design, you have to have two treatments only. You can't do it with three, four, five treatments. So, if you got more treatments, then this design is just out of the question. But, the idea is you pair two people together because they have a lot a lot of shared attributes. So, maybe two females that are both in their 60s, both with severe Alzheimer's, two males, both in their 80s, both with severe Alzheimer's, and two men, both maybe younger with mild severity of Alzheimer's. Then, within each pair, randomly, of course, one gets the new medication, one gets the placebo. How you going to determine which gets which? Again, randomly. It's got to be random, right? Now, you have many, many, many pairs. So, with 100 subjects, we pair and make 50 pairs. And at the end, hey, guess what? We still got 50 people taking the new medication. We still got 50 people taking the placebo. But, the idea is that the data is paired together so we can really hone in on these two individuals are extremely, extremely similar. The only difference is one got the new medication one got the placebo. And then, we can really start to analyze, "Hey, everybody getting the new medication got better. It really does work regardless of gender, regardless of age, regardless of severity of Alzheimer's." You can also match people up with themselves. Might not really work in this example, but imagine that you're doing something with suntan lotion and you could pair me up with me. One arm gets the new suntan lotion, one arm gets an old suntan lotion or some type of placebo, and then I can really compare them as well. So, people can even be paired up with themselves. Twins also make great pairs because they're even the same down to the genetic level if that really matters for your research question. So, those are the three different types of designs that you could use. All of them are great, and again, it's specific in terms of what your population is and what your research question is. What are some of the key things that can be asked of you on the AP exam when it comes to experimental design? Well, one is that typically one of the six FRQs is always about some type of experiment. Again, not always, but very common. And things they can ask are, "Hey, you know, what's the exploratory variable? What are the different treatments? What's the response variable? Who are the experimental units?" All those kind of basic questions. They could ask you to design a study or recognize what type of design is being utilized. They can even give you a matched pair design that's not designed properly and ask how maybe you would fix it to make it better. And they also love to focus on why it would be important to do a block study. Again, a block experimental study is designed when you have another variable that you know is going to impact your response and you don't want it to. They love to ask you questions, so you can make sure you focus on those things, whether it be multiple choice where you're choosing which design is best. You know, there might even be a multiple-choice question focusing on a matched pair design, and one design says, "Hey, you're going to pair up two people up because they're very, very different." Another one says you're going to pair people together because they're very, very similar, but they both get the same treatment. Things like that are all bad. Remember that in matched pair design, we want to pair people together because they're extremely similar, but one of them randomly gets one treatment and one gets the other treatment. So, questions like that are very, very common. The last major topic in this unit is over statistical inference in experiments. Statistical inference is a huge word that we're going to use a ton throughout the entire course. But the idea of inference is very simple. Inference is the idea of using data from a sample usually we call data from a sample statistics and trying to use those data, use those statistics to make judgments about the larger population from which it came from. In a simple sentence, that's what inference is all about. Using data from a sample to make judgments about what could be true for a larger population from which that data came from. Now, the big rule here is that if you want to be able to use data from a sample to make a judgment about a larger population which it came from, that data must be selected randomly. As long as that data was selected randomly and it's representative of that population, then you can certainly use that data to make generalizations to the larger population from which it came from. Another big vocabulary word here is statistically significant results. We see it, we hear it all the time. After a research study was done, we found that there was statistically significant evidence that X impacts Y or X causes Y. What does statistically significant evidence mean? Here's the idea. When you are done with an experiment, right? You are going to have two or more sets of data. And guess what? I guarantee you that those two or more sets of data are going to be different. Why? Because they came from different samples or different groups. They're guaranteed to be different. The question is, why are they different? If they're only a little bit different, then maybe it was just because of chance. So, maybe we have a group of freshmen and we take measure how long it takes them to do their homework every week. We have a group of seniors and we measure how long it takes them to do their homework every week. And and the difference is very small. In fact, it's so small that it, you know what? It was just by pure chance. If we did the research again with a different group of freshmen, a different group of seniors, then maybe the seniors spend more time or maybe the freshmen may spend more time. And it's just such a small difference that it's just by pure chance and it doesn't really mean anything. Freshmen don't spend more, seniors don't spend less. But imagine if the difference is really, really big. So big that it could not have occurred by chance. Well, then in that situation, we have what we call statistically significant results. Meaning that the difference was so big it could not have occurred by chance. So, if we got our freshmen, we got our seniors, and the difference between them was really, really big in terms of how much they spend on homework, well, then we could maybe possibly conclude that seniors spend less time on homework or freshmen spend more time. Because if we were to repeat that over and over and over and over again, freshmen, seniors, freshmen, seniors, in every single situation and every single sample, we would get the freshmen being more and the seniors being less. That would result in statistically significant evidence. Even at the end of an experiment, we have our Alzheimer's patients with the new medication, we have our Alzheimer's patients with the placebo. If the difference in their memory scores is significant, statistically significant, then that tells us that the difference could not have occurred by chance. Which, guess what? Means the medication worked. That would be our conclusion. So, when you claim that you have statistically significant results, you're saying that the differences that you're seeing between your sets of data are too big that they could not have occurred by chance. Now, when we have statistically significant results, well, then that could lead us, if everything is done correctly, to say that one variable caused another variable to change. So, if everything about my Alzheimer's patients experiment was perfect and I have statistically valid results, then what I could say is that, "Hey, I have data that supports the fact that the new medication will cause an increase in memory for patients with Alzheimer's like the ones that I used." But, something really important here to note is if you go back and listen to everything I've been saying, I said that the 100 patients in that Alzheimer's experiment were volunteers. And you may say, "Oh, wait a minute, you told us earlier that volunteers are really bad." Yes, if you're doing a sample survey and you're collecting a sample using volunteers is terrible. But, oftentimes in an experiment, you sometimes have to use volunteers because you can't force people to take a medication. So, you get volunteers who are willing to do it. All that means is that who I can generalize results my results to is kind of limited. Meaning that I can only say that this new medication will work for people that are similar to those that were in my experiment. Similar to those that volunteered. I can't say that it's going to work for everybody out there that has Alzheimer's because I used volunteers. If I wanted to be able to generalize my results to all people that have Alzheimer's, then I would need to make sure that I choose the 100 subjects randomly, forcing them to take the medication, which could be a little bit unethical. But, those are just some of the drawbacks when you're working with experiments. That's what this section is all about. The idea of what has to happen for you to infer your results to the larger population. Got to be a random sample for sure. And if it's an experiment, you have to have that random assignment. Maybe you use volunteers and that's okay for the most part. But, it just limits who you can apply your results to. You can only say it's true for people similar to the volunteers that you have. Statistically significant results means that any observed differences in our sets of data could not have occurred by chance. And that they are too large to just be because of pure happenstance. They must have occurred for some reason, and that would lead us to conclude in an experiment that one variable caused a change in the other variable. Now, what does statistically significant mean? Well, it means exactly that, that the results were too big to have occurred by chance. Random assignment in experiment is the only way that we can be led to statistically significant results. Who gets what has to be selected randomly, or else you'll never get statistically significant results. What are the key questions that could be asked of you on the AP test here? Well, honestly, a lot of it hinders upon who or what you can generalize your results to. That's why you really got to focus on the key concepts. If you're just doing some type of observational study where you're collecting data to learn about a larger population, the data you collect has to be random in order to generalize those results, but you can only generalize those results to specific population from which your data was taken from. Now, if you want to be able to claim that one thing causes another through an experiment, and you want those results to be statistically significant, then you have to have that random assignment in your experiment. In terms of who gets what treatment has to be done randomly. Now, one thing that typically happens in an experiment is you have to use volunteers because you can't forcibly make randomly selected people do something if they don't well want to do it. So, typically, experiments do use volunteers, and that's still okay. The only drawback there is that we can only apply our results to people similar to the volunteers in our study. We can't apply results to all people from that entire population, unless the people in your experiment were randomly selected first and then randomly assigned their treatments, which, I'll be honest, is kind of rare because it's hard to force people to do something if they don't want to.
