Hey statsars, welcome to the unit two part A summary review video covering analyzing two categorical variables and probability. Before we get started, I want to mention one quick thing. The AP Statistics course and description has recently been updated. So, some of the topic numbers and units I reference throughout this video may not perfectly match the new curriculum. However, the good news is that the content itself hasn't changed. The concepts, vocabulary, definitions, statistical techniques, and problem-solving skills covered in this video are all still essential for success in AP Statistics. So, if you're looking for a comprehensive review of analyzing two categorical variables and basic probability, you're in the right place. Everything you need to know is right here. So, sit back, grab your notes, and let's review the key concepts together. And don't forget to check out the ultimate review packet for AP Statistics. Inside, you'll find study guides, guided notes, practice worksheets, review videos, answer keys, and many other resources designed to help you prepare for your unit test throughout the year and ultimately for success on the AP Statistics exam in May. So, let's get started. First, let's start with analyzing one set of data where we collect two categorical variables from that set of data. The best way to organize and understand our data is with what's called a two-way table. Let's take a look at one now. Here's a two-way table where we looked at a sample of students and we asked them two questions. First, how did you get to school? What was your mode of transportation? And second, were you tardy at least once in the last week? From a table like this, we could calculate some sample statistics. Now, why are they called sample statistics? Well, because they come from a sample. Remember, statistics is the word that we use for any information that is taken from a sample. We have several different types of statistics that we can gather from one of these two-way tables for a categorical variable. First what we have are what are called marginal relative frequencies. Here, we're looking at the values that are in the totals. That's why that's called marginal because the totals are in the margins of the table. We could either look at the proportion of all the kids that were tardy at least once that week, or the proportion of kids that were not tardy at least once that week. We could also look at the margins for the different types of transportation, like the proportion of kids that rode the bus, the proportion of kids that walked to school, the proportion of kids that drive themselves to school, or the proportion of kids that get a ride from their parent to school. But again, we calculate these values from the totals by dividing it by the grand total, hence the marginal relative distributions. Next up, we have what's called joint relative frequencies. When we say the word joint, we basically mean the word and. So, we're combining any two of the categories together. For example, we could look at the proportion of kids that were tardy and rode the bus. Or we could look at the proportion of kids that walked to school and drive themselves to school. For a joint relative frequency, we're looking at a specific value that matches for those two variables in the table, and again dividing by the grand total. Lastly, we have conditional relative frequencies. These are a little bit more confusing, but once you get the hang of it, it's really simple. The idea is there's a condition attached, and when there's a condition attached, it changes the value in the denominator. For example, you'll see given a student is tardy, what proportion of them ride the bus to school? So, the condition is that it's given that they were tardy. That means our denominator is only limited to looking at the kids that were tardy because it was given, right? There was a condition attached. We know that the student that we're talking about was tardy to school. So, our denominator becomes much, much smaller because we're only looking at those students that were tardy, and of those tardy students, how many of them rode the bus to school? So, we take how many rode the bus to school and were tardy, and we divide it by the total that were tardy to get our conditional relative frequency. Another example we see here is given that they walked to school, what proportion of them tardy? This time the condition, what is given is that they walk to school. Once again, that limits our denominator to only looking at kids that walk to school, cuz that's the condition, that's what we know. The numerator is both walking to school and tardy. So, take that value divided by the total of kids that walk to school, and you'll have your conditional relative frequency. Hopefully, that makes a lot of sense. For all three of those statistics that we looked at, marginal, joint, and conditional, notice that we looked at the relative frequencies. We didn't look at just the counts or how many. Why is that? Because when you have unequal sample sizes, or again, a different amount of kids that walk to school, different amount of kids that take the bus to school, different amount of kids that are tardy, different amount of kids that are not tardy, it makes more sense to compare proportions or percentages, or what's relative to the whole. If we just look at counts, it's not really fair because some, you know, more kids might take the bus to school, less kids might walk to school. So, just looking at counts isn't really a fair comparison. But, if we're going to compare probabilities and proportions within these tables, it makes a way more sense to look at relative frequencies, which are proportion-based. Two-way tables can also be turned into some pretty sweet graphs called segmented bar graphs. Here, we see a segmented bar graph where we've broken down the bars by mode of transportation. And then, within each mode of transportation, we're separating the proportion of kids that were tardy and the proportion of kids that were not tardy in that mode. So, basically, we're looking at conditional relative frequencies here, because the first bar is only for those kids that ride the bus, and of those kids, what proportion are tardy, what proportion are not. So, we can see the breakdown for each mode of transportation. We can also switch that around, and now have each bar representing the proportion of kids that are tardy and the kids that are not tardy. So, the first bar we see here is all the kids that were tardy, and then the breakdown of what proportion of them ride the bus, what proportion of them walk to school, what proportion of them drive to school, and what proportion of them get a ride from their parent. And then we also see the same thing in the bar that's marked the total kids that were not tardy. Again, each segment is the proportion of the different modes of transportation. So we could switch it up and it's all provided the same amount of information. It's really just a matter of what the condition is. Is the condition the mode of transportation, or is the condition being tardy or not? That would determine how you create the segmented bar graph. But segmented bar graphs show the proportions within each of those conditions. They make for great ways to display the data and really understand what's going on within the data. Now, the really important thing we want to do here is determine if there is any type of an association or relationship between these variables of mode of transportation and being tardy or not. Now, to do this we really have to take a look at the marginal relative frequencies versus the conditional relative frequencies. Now, please note when we say that there is an association between two variables, we mean that they are not independent. An association means that one variable has something to do with the other variable and vice versa. If two variables are independent, they actually have no association whatsoever, which means one variable does not impact the other. Let's dive into this example to talk about if there is an association or not. Here's how we can actually explore the relationship between these two quantitative variables. So first we want to take a look at the marginal relative frequency. For example, what proportion were tardy at least once? So here we see the marginal again, 65 out of 188 kids, or about 34.6% of all the students surveyed were tardy at least once. Then we want to do is we want to compare to that the conditional relative frequencies. So meaning if we only look at the kids that rode the bus. So of the 35 kids that rode their bus, 12 were tardy. That's about 34% of the 29 kids that went for their parents to school, 10 were tardy, it's about 34% as well. Of the kids that drove themselves to school, it's about 31 out of 90, again, also about 34% and then finally of the 34 kids that walked to school, we saw about 12 that were tardy or about 35%. So, what we're noticing is that you know, we start off with that marginal, about 34% of kids are tardy to school at least once during the week, but notice that if you ride the bus, about 34% tardy, if you drive to school with the parent, about 34% tardy, if you drive yourself to school, about 34% tardy, and if you walk to school, pretty darn close to about 34% are tardy. This is a sign of no association because what we're showing here is that again, start off with that marginal, 34% of kids are tardy. However, if you arrive to school by bus, it didn't go up, it didn't go down, it stayed about 34%. If you drove with the parent, it didn't go up, it didn't go down, it stayed about 34%. If you drove yourself, it did not go up, it did not go down, it stayed at about 34%. Yeah, walking I know is a teeny bit more, it's about 35.3%, but again, it's really, really close to 34%. So, what we're seeing is that the added conditions of how you got to school did not make it any more or less likely for you to be tardy. It's all around 34-ish percent. This is an a sign of an association. When you attach a condition to a question and it doesn't change the marginal relative frequency, well then there's no association. Clearly, how you arrive to school does not make you any more likely or less likely to be tardy. Now, that's in stark contrast to looking at this example. So, here I'm using the same concept, the same idea, being tardy yes or no, and how you got to school, but I changed the numbers a little bit. But first I want you to notice that the grand totals are all the same. It's the numbers within the table that I made different. Now, in this example, we're going to see that there is a clear association. For example, once again, we start off with the marginal relative frequency, where we see about 65 out of 188 kids were tardy to school, again, about that 34%. But this time, when we look at the kids that rode the bus, of the 35 kids that rode the bus, only four were tardy. Wow, it dropped to 11%. So again, something about riding the bus made you less likely, or there's less of a chance that you are tardy. If you drove with a parent, five out of those 29 kids were tardy, about 17%, also a little bit less than that marginal value. And if you drove yourself, of the 90 kids that drove themselves, 48 were tardy. That is now up to 53%. So we see that if you drive yourself to school, you are much more likely to be tardy. And then lastly, of the kids that walked to school, eight were tardy, and that's at about 24%. So here we're seeing very different numbers. The marginal relative frequency, that 34% number, is not matching up with the conditionals. That is a sign of an association, because this is showing that when you add that added condition of, hey, I I drive, you know, I go on the bus to get to school, it made me less likely to be tardy. And if I drive myself to school, it made me much more likely to be tardy. This is where we see that there is an association, because those conditions changed the probabilities, or changed the proportions. We can also see this concept of an association or not in the segmented bar graphs. Here's the second segmented bar graphs for our data that showed no association. Notice, no matter how you get to school, bus, parent, self, or walk, about 34%, that's the bluish, that are tardy and about 66%, that's the purple that are not tardy. Again, showing that it doesn't change. Driving the bus, going with your parents, driving yourself, doesn't change those proportions, which means no association. Well, here is the segmented bar graphs for the example where there is association. We see that when you drive yourself to school, there is a change and a noticeable change in the proportion of kids that are tardy versus proportion of kids that are not tardy. So, we don't see the exact same numbers across the board. When you see the exact same numbers across the board, 34, 34, 34, 34%, that's the sign of no association. When you see a change of those conditional relative frequencies, that's a sign that there is an association. So, make sure you know how to establish these these concepts by looking at the segmented bar graphs or by looking at the two-way tables and then writing up a nice explanation of that. Now, from this section over analyzing two categorical variables measured from one set of data, what are some big things are going to come up on your exam or on the AP exam? Well, the major things we're looking at are just calculating some of those different statistics that I mentioned. Can you calculate marginal distributions, joint distributions, or conditional distributions? Just being able to find those values in a two-way table is really, really simple and really, really easy to do, but it's definitely going to be asked of you. The second thing you're going to have to be able to do is answer that question, is there an association from looking at either a two-way table or looking at a segmented bar graph? That's a big concept, a big idea that's definitely going to come up on the AP exam for sure. Understanding probability begins with understanding what a random process is. A random process generates results that are random or simply unknown and determined by chance. Seeing patterns through collection of data through a random process doesn't necessarily mean that the variation we see is not random. Random just means unknown and uncertain. An outcome is the result of a random process, and an event is a collection of outcomes. Now, when we say that an outcome is random, all we're saying is that we don't yet know what that outcome will be. For example, if the random process is rolling a fair six-sided die, and we say that an outcome is random, all that means is we don't yet know what that outcome will be until we roll the die. Probability is simply trying to quantify the uncertainty in a random process. One viewpoint of the probability of an outcome is the long-run relative frequency of that outcome occurring. Meaning, after a large number of repetitions of that random process, we could take the number of times we observe the outcome occur, divided by the total number of repetitions ran. We can find these long-run relative frequencies, also known as probabilities, through simulation. Take rolling a die. If we want to find the probability of rolling a two, we could start tossing a die repeatedly. And after a large number of tosses, we could count the number of times we observed a two, and divided by the total number of times we tossed the die in total. But, what is the long run? If we only rolled the die 20 times and counted the total number of times we observed a two divided by 20, we wouldn't get a very accurate probability. This is where the law of large numbers comes in. The law of large numbers states that simulated probabilities tend to get closer to the true probability the more trials we perform. Basically, if we want to be more accurate, we need to run more trials. But, how many is many? Well, a lot, infinite. That is why the long-run relative frequency definition of probability will never be perfect, because we can never run infinite simulations or infinite trials. So, just know that the more trials we run, the more accurate we will be to what the true probability could be. Now, rolling a die is a really simple random process to repeat over and over, where other random processes are not so easy to actually simulate. For example, let's just say the manager of a fast food restaurant knows that through past observations, 65% of customers who ask for a free cup for water take that free cup to the soda fountain and fill with something other than water. I know, a crime. Now, maybe he wants to find the probability that of the next 10 customers who ask for a free cup of water, four or less of them fill with something other than water. Now, how would he actually simulate this random process? He would have to look at 10 customers, record how many actually filled their water cup up with something other than water, and then redo redo it again, and do it again, and do it again, and then he's going to count how many of those trials resulted in four or less people filling their free water cup up with something other than water, but that's really not feasible for the manager to actually do a large number of times. So, what could he do? Well, he could actually run a simulation using numbers. Running a simulation with numbers is where we pretend that numbers through a random number table are customers, and some numbers are going to designate that those customers are going to fill their water cup up with something other than water, while other numbers would represent that those customers are filling their water cup up with, well, water. Then we could look at 10 customers and record how many of them fill up their water cup with something other than water, look at another 10 customers, another 10 customers, another 10 customers, and because we're using numbers through a random number table or a random number generator instead of actually looking at customers, we could do this really quickly and really fast, and actually generate some good probabilities. Then after many many repetitions of looking at these 10 numbers, we could then count how many of our trials had four or less people fill their water cup up with something in the water, divide by a total number of trials, and we could get an estimated probability. But again, we would need to run a large number of trials to get close to the true probability of this event. Simulations can be very useful to try to find, or at least begin to find what the true probability of an event is, but through some basic rules that I could teach you, we could calculate probabilities, true probabilities, really easily and really quickly. All we have to do is think. To find the true probability of an event without a simulation, we need to first understand a couple basic ideas and some ground rules when it comes to probability. The sample space of a chance process is a list of all non-overlapping outcome outcomes. For example, the sample space for the random process of rolling a fair six-sided die would be 1 2 3 4 5 6. And we typically label observable events with letters. For example, A equals we see a five on the next roll, or B equals we see a two on the next roll, or C equals we see an even number on the next roll. Now, finding the probability of event A, seeing a five on the next roll, is actually quite easy. All we got to do is take the total number of outcomes that are in favor of event A in the numerator, divided by in the denominator the total number of outcomes in the sample space. And for this situation, that'd be one outcome that's in favor of rolling a five, divided by six total outcomes in the sample space, for a probability of 1/6. But you already knew that. The probability of an event is always between zero and one, inclusive, meaning it could be zero or could be one or any number in between. Now, the complement of an event is that the event does not happen. And to find the probability of the complement, all you have to do is do one minus the probability that the event will happen. Makes it really simple. So, if the probability that event A occurring is 50%, then you should automatically know very quick without doing a lot of math that the probability that event A does not happen is 85%. Next, we have the probability of event A and event B occurring. This is looking for the probability that both events A and B occur at the same time, and we can express this probability in two ways. This is also known as joint probability. Now, if the two events cannot happen at the same time, then we call them disjoint or mutually exclusive. And if two events are in fact mutually exclusive, then we automatically know that the probability of A and B is a big, beautiful zero. Next, we have the probability of the event A occurring or the probability of event B occurring, and we can express this using two different ways as well. This is known as the union of event A and event B. Now, when we're asked to find the probability of A or B, what we're really looking for is the probability that event A only happens, or the probability of event B only happens, or the probability that both A and B happen. Now, the complement of the probability of A or B is the probability that neither event happens. So, if you think about two events A or B, there's four things that can happen. A only happens, B only happens, A and B happens, or neither happen. And when we talk about the probability of the union or A or B happening, then we're looking at three of those things. A happens, B happens, or both. That's why the opposite is neither. Now, find the probability of event A and B occurring, or the probability of event A or B occurring, often times drives students bonkers. Well, I can make it as easy as I possibly can, but first we have to talk about conditional probability. Conditional probability is when you are asked to find the probability of event A occurring given or on the condition that event B has already or will occur. Now, we express conditional probability like this. Before the line is what we're trying to find the probability of and after the line is the event or the thing that we already know that has occurred or has been given to occur. And we have a really simple formula probability of A on the condition of B. All we got to do is take the probability of A and B divided by the probability of B, the condition. So, regardless of what letters we use, just know that the probability for conditional probability or the formula for conditional probability is to take the probability of both events happening at the same time in the numerator divided by the probability of the condition in the denominator. Okay, now we've gone through enough concepts of basic probability that we can talk about a couple really big probability rules. The first major rule is called the addition rule and it helps us find the probability of A or B. Here's how the formula for it works. To find the probability of A or B, we take the probability of A plus the probability of B and we subtract the probability of A and B. In simple terms, the word or means to add when it comes to probability. But you have to consider that if the two events have some overlap, meaning they're not mutually exclusive, you have to subtract away the overlap, which is the probability of A and B. Now, for some this could be hard to follow. Recall the probability of A or B is the probability of only A, only B, as well as the probability of A and B. So, if the two outcomes A and B have some overlap, again, not mutually exclusive, that means inside of the probability of A is some overlap with the probability of B, and inside of the probability of B is some overlap with the probability of A. Now, we want this overlap to count, but we don't want the overlap to count twice. Seeing this through a Venn diagram makes it really easy to understand. If the events are mutually exclusive, there is no overlap. That is to say the probability of A and B equals zero. So, we see two non-interlinking circles, so the probability of A or B makes complete sense. It's simply the probability of A plus the probability of B. If the events are not mutually exclusive, there is overlap. If we were to simply add the probability of A plus the probability of B, then the overlap that is in both gets counted twice. That is why in the addition rule, we subtract the probability of A and B away. We subtract the overlap, not to get rid of it, but to stop it from being double-counted. So, when we subtract, we get the probability of only A, only B, and A and B. The second huge [snorts] big rule is called the multiplication rule. The multiplication rule states to find the probability of A and B, you take the probability of A times the probability of B given that A has already occurred. In simple terms, the word and in probability means to multiply, but you must always consider how the second event is impacted by the first event, which is why conditional probability is needed. Probabilities of multiple events are as simple as multiplying the probabilities together, as long as you consider how the probabilities are affected as the event progresses. This brings me to the final major topic of introductory probability, and that is independence between events. Two events, A and B, are independent if and only if knowing that event A has occurred or will occur does not change or impact the probability that event B will occur. Basically, two events are independent if the probability of A equals the probability of A on the condition of B. And the probability of B equals the probability of B on the condition of A. If the probability of A is the same regardless of if event B occurs or not, then the events are independent. This brings me back to the multiplication rule. To find the probability of A and B, and you know that A and B are independent, then all you have to do is take the probability of A times the probability of B. But that's only if they're independent because they're not affecting each other. But if you know that they are not independent or you're unsure, to find the probability of A and B, you must take the probability of A times the probability of B given that A has already occurred. By now you may be pretty confused, but here's the one big recommendation I have. You can never go wrong if you write out everything that can happen explicitly, and at that point usually probabilities are pretty easy to figure out. But, to try to get through some of the confusion, let's take a look at a couple AP-style probability questions and make sense of them using our rules. All right, the first probability question we're going to take a look at deals with a two-way table. Now, the AP Statistics exam will love working with two-way tables and probability. You'll see a couple of them scattered throughout the multiple choice, and you might even see one on the free response section. All right, here's the problem that we're going to take a look at. We surveyed 500 high school students asking them two questions. What high school do you go to? High school A, high school B, high school C. And what is your favorite flavor of slushy? Red, blue, green, or neither. Now, with a two-way table we can ask you so many different probability questions. So, let's begin. The first question is one student is selected at random from those surveyed. What is the probability that the student is from high school A? Now, we got to remember the basic probability probability rule, right? On the numerator goes the number of outcomes in favor of what we're looking for, high school A, that's 140 students divided by the total number of students in the sample space, in that case it's 500. 140 divided by 500 is 0.28 or 28%. So, the probability that a student is picked and that student goes to high school A is 28%. Second question is, again, a student is picked at random from those surveyed. What is the probability that this student prefers a red slushy? Well, once again, in our numerator is the number of kids that meet or are favored to what we're looking for, that's 275 kids we can clearly see in the two-way table that prefer a red slushy out of the total sample space of 500 students and we get 55% of students surveyed love a red slushy. The third question posed us, once again, student selected at random from those surveyed. What is the probability that that student loves a red slushy and is from high school A? Once again, remember we see that a word and we're looking for what's called joint probability. So, we're just looking at where that row and column are joined together. So, we see that there's a total of 91 students that are both in high school A and love an orange excuse me, orange red slushy. And again, you don't have to do any sophisticated math to figure that out. All you got to do is look where those two rows and columns are joined together. 91 out of 500 is 18.2%. Now, the fact that we have 18.2% of kids that both go to high school A and love a red slushy means that the two events, go to high school A and loving a red slushy, are not mutually exclusive because we literally have 91 kids that do both. And our next question, once again, starts off with given that a student is going to be picked at random from those surveys, this time it's what is the probability that that student is from high school A or likes a red slushy. Now, I can also say it the other way around. What's the probability that they like a red slushy or a high school or from high school A? That doesn't matter the order there. Now, remember, in basic probability, really simple way, or means add. But we've got to make sure that if they're not mutually exclusive, which we just said they weren't, that we subtract away any overlap. So, to find the probability, we're going to start off with the probability that a student loves a red slushy, 275 out of 500. We're going to add the probability that the student comes from high school A, 140 out of 500. But 91 kids just got double counted. See, 91 kids were counted amongst the 275 that like a red slushy, and 91 kids were also counted amongst those that are from high school A. Those 91 kids that both are from high school A and love red slushies deserve to be counted, but they don't deserve to be double counted. why we subtract away the overlap. Again, we're not getting rid of them. We're just preventing them from being double counted. So, once we do the math, we've got a common denominator. It's pretty simple, 324 out of 500 or 64.8%. Next question from this two-way table is what is the probability that the student prefers a red slushy, but there's a condition, given that the student is from high school A. So, I noticed I capitalized and I underlined and I bolded the word given. But again, you got to make sure that you understand when you see that word given, you're working with a conditional probability. So, we're trying to find the probability that the person likes the red slushy, but then we have our line for the given, and then after line is the condition that they come from high school A. Now, don't forget we have a pretty simple formula for conditional probability. The numerator is the probability of both things, A and B, in this case red slushy and from high school A, divided by the probability of the condition, which is they're from high school A. So, following the math in the numerator is the probability that they are both from high school A and love red slushies. Once again, that's 91 kids out of 500 divided by the probability they are from high school A, which is 140 divided by 500. If you're really good at math and dividing with fractions, you'll see that those 500s are going to cancel. So, we get 91 out of 140 or 65%. Now, you actually don't even need the conditional probability for this formula or for this specific problem if you truly understand conditional probability. So, the idea is that we're given that the student is from high school A. That means we're only allowed to look at that row for high school A. We're not allowed to look at the total cuz that got kids from high school B and high school C. We obviously don't want to look at the rows for B and C cuz we're given that they must be from high school A and that changes our denominator to 140. We're only allowed to look at those 140 kids from high school A and of those kids from high school A, 91 love the red slushy, so 91 out of 140. So, you can logically think through this problem to get your answer or you can certainly use the conditional probability formula and it still works to get your final answer 65%. Now, the last type of question that the AP test loves to ask when working with a two-way table is is there independence between two events. So, here we're going to stick with the two events going to high school A and liking a red slushy. And are these two events independent? Well, it's really simple cuz we have a formula that we could check to see if they're independent or not. And it's really simple. In generic terms, remember to check if two events A and B are independent, we have to find the probability of A and compare it to the probability of A given B. If they're equal, then it just goes to show that B didn't matter at all, hence they're independent. So, here what we're going to do is we're going to find the probability that the student loves a red slushy, which we've actually already did. We already know that that's 55% 275 out of 500 kids love a red slushy, no condition attached there whatsoever. Then we're going to compare that to the probability that the student loves a red slushy given that they're from high school A. So, we want to do here is we want to see does being from high school A change the probability of somebody loving a red slushy? If it changes it, then we have two And well, we just got done actually finding that conditional probability. Again, remember the conditions that they're from high school A, so there's 140 total students from high school A, and of those 140 students, 91 love red slushies. 91 out of 140 is 65%. So, what we see here is that there's a 55% probability of a student preferring a red slushy. However, if the student is from high school A, the probability they prefer a red slushy is 65%. So, being from high school A makes a student more likely to prefer a red slushy, not independent. If one event changes the probability of another, then they clearly somehow depend on each other and they're intermixed, so they cannot be independent. So, in this situation, the two events loving red slushies and being from high school A are certainly not independent. Now, obviously I focus on being from high school A and liking red slushies for this problem, but I could have worked on blue slushies, green slushies, high school B, high school C, or any combination of that to ask some really good probability questions from a two-way table. Now, we're going to take a look at a couple what I call generic probability questions cuz we just have generic events A and B with no real-world problems or words attached to them. But the AP test loves to put these on the multiple-choice questions just to make sure that you know how the different formulas work with each other. So, let's take a look at a couple. In this first generic problem, we are given two events A and B. And we're told a couple things. First, the probability of A is 35% and the probability of event B is 20% and we're told that we need to find the probability of A or B knowing that A and B are mutually exclusive. Okay, first we're going to start off with the fact that they're asking us to find the probability of A or B. So, we know we're going to need the our or formula, one of the most important formulas when it comes to probability. The probability of A or B equals the probability of A plus the probability of B minus the probability of A and B. Now, the next step comes at other super important piece of information that they are mutually exclusive. Mutually exclusive means that the two events have no outcomes in common and cannot happen at the same time, which instantly tells us that the probability of A and B is a beautiful zero. So, now we know everything we got to do to fill in the formula. The probability of A is 0.35 plus the probability of B is 0.20 minus the probability of A and B, which we just said is zero because of the fact of mutually exclusive, and that makes the math really, really simple. We're just going to take 0.35 and add 0.20 to get 0.55 or 55%. So, the probability of A or B is 55%. And this next problem has a very similar setup. We're given two events, A and B. The probability of A is 0.35, the probability of B is 0.20, and we're asked to find the probability of A or B. But this time, it tells us that A and B are independent. All right, so where do we begin? Well, once again, us to find the probability of A or B. So, we're going to need that or formula. The probability of A or B is the probability of A plus the probability of B minus the probability of A and B. So, we can easily fill in the A with 0.35 and the B with 0.20. Now, what do we know is true when the probability of two events, or excuse me, when two events are independent? Well, when A and B are independent, it means that to find the probability of A and B, all you got to do is multiply the probability of A times the probability of B. Now, normally we got to think about, well, does A change B? But remember, when they're independent, it doesn't. A and B don't affect each other, and that is why when they're independent, we know that the probability of A and B is automatically the probability of A times the probability of B. So, we could actually, since we already know the probability of A and B, we could easily find the probability of A and B. All we got to do is take 0.35, multiply by 0.20 to get 0.07. So, now the probability of A or B is 0.35 for the probability of A plus 0.20 for the probability of B minus 0.07 for the probability of A and B. Put all that together, we get a final answer of 0.48 or 48. Or excuse me, 48%. Now, this next problem looks really similar, but it's a little bit different. And because it's a little bit different, it's going to make us have to do some dreaded algebra. But thank goodness, the algebra doesn't get too difficult. So, once again, we're given two events, A and B, and we're told the probability of A is .35 and the probability of A or B is .80 and we're asked to find the probability of B and in this first problem we're told that A and B are mutually exclusive. All right, so this time we know the probability of A or B, we know the probability of A and we're asked to find the probability of B. So since we know the probability of A or B, we're still going to want to use that or formula. The probability of A or B equals the probability of A plus the probability of B minus the probability of A and B. Now, the other information is super important that they're mutually exclusive which automatically tells us that the probability of A and B is a beautiful zero. So now we just got to fill everything in. This time we already know the probability of A or B is .80 equals the probability of A .35 plus the probability of B which we don't know so we can either turn it into an X which which you which is which you would do in algebra or you can just leave it as a probability of B minus zero because the probability of A and B is zero when you're mutually exclusive. Now we got a really simple algebra equation to solve. All we got to do is subtract the .35 to the other side to get .45. So the probability of B is .45. And our final generic question looks like this. Given two events A and B, the probability of A is .35, the probability of A or B is .80. We're asked to find the probability of B but this time A and B are independent. All right, this one involves a little bit more algebra so hopefully you can stay with me here. So first once again we know the probability of A or B but we're still going to use that formula. The probability of A plus the probability of B minus probability of A and B. Now we already know the probability of A or B so we can substitute that in as .80. We already know the probability of A so we can substitute that in as .35 and we're asked to find the probability of B so we can either just leave that as a probability of B or we can change it to an X if you really want to make this look like algebra. Now what does it mean when the two events are independent? Well once again when two events are independent it means that the probability of A and B is simply the probability of A times the probability of B. We don't have to think about any kind of conditional probabilities. So, we know the probability of A is 0.35 and we're going to multiply that by the probability of B, which we don't know. So, we end up with this equation, 0.80 = 0.35 + the probability of B - 0.35 * the probability of B. Now, what we have to recognize here is again algebra. We have some like terms. We have one probability of B - 0.35 probability of B. Those are like terms. And if that's a little bit confusing to you, just turn them into X's. Make the probability of B an X, so we have 1X - 0.35 * X, cuz again probability of B is an X. So, once again, those are like terms that we can simply combine. One probability of B - 0.35 probability of B is 0.65 probability of B. Just combining like terms with algebra here. Doesn't get too difficult. Then we have again a pretty basic algebra equation to solve. We're going to subtract the 0.55 from the 0.80 to get 0.45. Then to solve the probability of B, we have to undo that multiplication with division. So, we're going to divide 0.65 to the other side and we get a final answer of 0.692 for the probability of B. So, a really good problem. They love putting ones like this on the AP test cuz it forces you to do a little bit of algebra with some good probability work. Now, here's one final probability question I want to ask you because almost every single year on the AP stats exam, there is a problem like this in some shape or form, whether it be on a multiple choice FRQ, but it's one that challenges students. But if you know how to do it, it's actually pretty easy. Here's the problem. 2% of a population of men have a disease. There is a test that could men could take for this disease, but no test in the medical field is perfect. So, if a man has the disease, the test will give a positive result saying they have the disease 95% of the time. If the man does not have the disease, the test will have a positive result 12% of the time. Well, that's called a false positive, cuz it says it's positive, but remember, the man doesn't have the disease. What is the probability a man has a positive result? Now, what a lot of students want to do right away is they just want to say, "Oh, 95% positive, 12% positive, add those together." But then you get a number bigger than 100%, which in probability world, that's impossible. So, let's take a look at this problem. Let's really think about all the different probabilities given. First, 2% probability that a man has the disease. Well, the complement would be the man does not have the disease, 98%. That's going to help us. Now, if the man has the disease, 95% of the time it'll have a positive result. That's a true positive, a positive that is accurate. But the complement of that would be the 5% chance that it comes back negative. That would be a false negative. It says you don't have the disease, but remember, you are you actually do. Then we have that 12% positive, but that's if you do not have the disease. That means a true negative would be the 88%. That's the complement of the 12%. 88% would be the probability that you have a negative result, when again, you do not have the disease. Now, let's walk through the different outcomes that are going to be what we want, a positive result. And that's going to get us the probability we're looking for. So, first, a man can have the disease, 2%, and the test comes back positive, 95%. So, just follow that through, 0.02 have the disease, and then there is a positive result, 0.95, and we get 0.019. Once again, why are we multiplying? Cuz I'm using the word and. And means multiply. And you can always multiply when you see the word and, as long as you think conditionally. That 95% is on the condition of the 2% happening first. That's why I had to write it that way. Now, the other scenario that leads to a positive result is you do not have the disease, 98%, and you still got that 12% chance of a positive result when you do not have the disease. So, once again, 0.98 * 0.12 = 0.1176. Now, only one of these situations can happen. They both can't happen. That would be They can't both happen at the same time. That'd be impossible. So, it's one or the other. And remember that word or means add. So, we're going to add those two final probabilities together to get a final answer for the probability of a positive result, 0.1366. Now, here is another very common question in a situation like this. Given that a man gets a positive result. So, a man goes to the doctor, says, "Hey, I want to I see if I have this disease or not." Doctor gives him a test. So, given that the man gets a positive result, test comes back positive, what is the probability he actually has the disease? Now, a lot of kids want a multiple-choice they're going to guess 2% because they go back to the problem. The problem said 2% chance they have the disease, and they're just going to answer that. But, yes, there is a 2% chance a man has a disease, but this is conditional. It says, "Given that they have a positive result." So, we want to find the probability the man has the disease given that they get a positive result. So, we are going to need our conditional probability formula, which is really easy, and it's on your formula sheet. In the numerator, we have the probability of both the disease and getting a positive result. In the denominator, we have the probability of simply the condition having a positive result. Numerator's really easy. So, have the disease and positive. Just follow that scenario. Have the disease, 0.02, and 0.95. Easy. Multiply, and we get our 0.019 the numerator. Now, the denominator is the probability of getting a positive result, which we just got done figuring out, 0.1366. Let me walk you through it one more time. Have the disease, 0.02, and test positive, 0.95. Multiply or cuz it can't both have the same time, so we're going to add. Not have the disease, 0.98, times still getting a positive result, 0.12. Multiply, add, we get that 0.1366 in the denominator. Then, we just have to divide everything, divide .019 by .1366 and we get our final answer of .1391. So, if a man gets a positive result, don't worry too much, you only have about a 14% chance of actually having the disease. That's still kind of high to be honest, but it's not like a positive result instantly means you're going to have the disease because so few people actually have the disease in the first place. Let's get started. Now, if a random process has outcomes of numerical values and those numerical values are uncertain, then we call it a random variable. Now, just know this. The outcomes of a random variable must be numerical values. Don't over complicate this. Just like an algebra class, a variable is a number that you don't know yet. Well, it's the same thing for a random variable. A random variable has an outcome that is a number that we just don't know what it is. But it could possibly be multiple different things based on uncertainty through that chance process. Now, there are two different types of random variables. Continuous random variables, which we'll talk about in unit five, so not right now. and discrete random variables which we're going to talk about right now in unit four. Now a discrete random variable is a variable that can take on a countable number of values. Each of those values of a random variable has a probability associated with it. And if you add up all those probabilities, you will get a sum of one. Now we typically use capital Latin letters like a, b, c, or x to represent a random variable. For a discrete random variable, we can make what's called a probability distribution. A probability distribution is a representation of every possible outcome of that discrete random variable and the probability associated with each of those outcomes. Now, we could either be given one of these probability distributions in the form of a graph or a table or we could use some information to create one on our own. Let's look at a couple examples. This first example of a random variable in creating a probability distribution for it involves horse racing. So, let's just say that you own a racehorse, and you're going to enter that raceh horse into two races. Now, here's what we know. The probability the horse wins the first race is 65%. If the horse wins the first race, the probability it wins the second race is 75%. However, if the horse loses the first race, the probability it wins the second race is 20%. Now, we're going to let X be the random variable how many races the horse wins. Now, why is this a discrete random variable? Because X is how many races the horse wins and that is a listable countable number of values. The horse could win zero races, the horse could win one race, or the horse could win two races. 0, one, and two are all listable, countable, and they're numerical. Now, to figure out the probabilities, because remember, probability distribution doesn't just have the list of possible outcomes for X. It also has the probabilities. We're going to need to learn a lot of the tactics that we learned back in the first video over unit 4 basic probability. Now the first thing I notice is that these are conditional probabilities because what happens for that second race all depends on what happens on that first race. Now to understand conditional probabilities it's best to make a tree diagram. So let's do that. So first the race is for race one and the horse enters it and we see that there's a 65% chance the horse wins that race which means automatically the compliment is a 35% chance the horse loses race one. Now here comes our conditional probabilities that were given to us. If the horse wins race one, so let's follow that 65%. There's a 75% chance the horse will then go on to win race two. The complement of that would be a 25% chance that the horse loses race two after the horse won race one. Then the horse could lose race one. So we're going to follow that 35% branch. Now we were told that the horse has a 20% chance to win race two if it lost race one. So that would be 20% and then the complement would be 80% chance that the horse loses race two if it already lost race one. Now to get to the end of any of these branches, all we have to do is multiply. And as long as we're understand that these are conditional probabilities. What happens for race two depends on race one, we are certainly allowed to multiply. So let's talk about the top branch where the horse wins two races. That's going to be 65 for the first race times 75 for the second race. Now that's going to give us a total of 4875 for the result of two wins. Now what about zero? Let's follow that bottom branch. No wins. 35 for the first race and then.80 for the second race. That's going to give us a total of 28. Now the other two branches are different. Lose the first, win the second or win the first, lose the second. They are different, which means they can't happen at the same time. But both of them have the same outcome of one win. So to find the probability of one win, we have to calculate each of those branches and add them together. So we have 65 win the first.25 lose the second or so. Now we're going to add the next option lose the first.35 times win the second.20. Add those options together and we get a grand total of 2325 for the probability of that the horse wins one race. So now we have a probability distribution for our discrete random variable X. We have our outcomes 0 1 and two and the associated probabilities. Now just note that we do because this is numerical we do like to put them in increasing order. Smallest at the top and then biggest at the bottom. So we like to go in that order of 0 1 and two. For our second example let's talk about having a game. And when a player plays this game the player could either get zero one two or three points on any given play of the game. Now here is a probability distribution in the form of a graph for this scenario. So across the bottom we see the outcomes 0 1 2 and three and then the graph shows us the probabilities for each of those outcomes. So we see the probability of getting zero points is 10%.1 probability of getting one point is 3% or excuse me 20%.2 probability of getting two points is 40%. 04 and the probability of getting three points is 30%.3. Now, this has a probability distribution already built for you. We already have the outcomes across the bottom and the associated probabilities. So, no real work for us to do here. Next, we can find the mean, which is referred to as the expected value of a random variable, and the standard deviation of a random variable to help us describe the center and the spread of a probability distribution. Let's start off talking about the mean. The mean is also known as the expected value. It's what we expect the outcome of the random variable to be in the long run after many, many, many, many trials or many, many, many passings of this random process of this random variable. Now, to find the expected value or the mean, it's actually not that complicated. All you have to do is take each outcome times its probability and then add all those together. So we could do that by hand. Outcome times probability plus outcome times its probability plus outcome times it probability dot dot dot dot dot. Or we also have this formula given to us on the APS stats formula sheet which use which tells us that the mean mu. Now remember why we're using that symbol mu because here we're talking about the long run which is essentially infinite, right? We're talking about the population or a parameter here. So we're saying that the mean, the expected value for this random variable X in the long run is well here it is. It's using this formula. So that giant looking E is sigma. So we're going to add together each individual outcome times that individual outcomes probability. Once we multiply and add all those together, we'll get our expected value. Now the standard deviation form is a little bit trickier. First, the standard deviation symbol is sigma. So the standard deviation of our random variable X equals All right, let me walk you through this here because it's going to be a little bit trickier than the mean. But what you do is you take each individual outcome minus the mean which you have to calculate first square that difference multiply by the probability of that individual. Now once you get done doing all that outcome minus the mean squared times the probability and you add all those together you get what we call variance. Then you square root all of that variance to get what we call standard deviation. Now, I know those two formulas for the mean and the standard deviation are a little bit tricky and a little bit overwhelming, but don't let that concern you. But perhaps what's most important than the formulas is actually understanding what these two values represent. The mean is the expected value of the random variable in the long run. So if we were to repeat the random variable many many many times, it's the expected or the average value we would expect in the long run. For example, if we said, "Hey, uh there's a new job opening. How many teachers do you expect to have to interview?" Well, that's what the mean of the random variable would be. Now, this purpose of the standard deviation is to give us an idea of the precision of that expected value. High values of a standard deviation relative to the expected value tell us that the expected value is well very imprecise. Whereas if we had low values of the standard deviation relative to the expected values, it would give us an opposite implication. Now the standard deviation gives an idea of a plus or minus range for the expected variable. So we could actually kind of find a interval for what outcomes of the random variable are most likely to occur by taking the mean going up and going down one standard deviation. All right, that's how easy it is to use your calculator to find the mean and standard deviation of a discrete random variable. First up is the binomial distribution. The binomial distribution is a probability distribution for a very special discrete random variable. Meaning that the outcomes are countable numerical values. It represents the random variable X, which equals the number of successes in n repeated trials. In other words, if a random process is repeated where only two things can happen on each trial, either success or fail, the binomial distribution counts the number of successes you will have in n opportunities or n trials. Now, what do I mean success? Well, a success is simply what we're looking for. It could be anything, good or bad. It's simply what we're looking to have happen on any specific trial. Now there are a few ground rules that we have to follow in order for a binomial distribution to work. First, what is success must be clearly defined and the probability of success must be given. We call the probability of success P. Also, the probability of success must stay the same on every single trial or opportunity. It cannot go up or down as the problem progresses. Now the other thing we automatically know when we have the probability of success is its complement which would be the probability of failure. So the probability of success is what we're looking to happen and then failure would be what we don't want to happen just the opposite of success. Now the next rule is that every single trial or opportunity must be independent of the next which means what happens on one trial whether it's success or fail cannot happen that cannot affect the next or the next or the next. Now the last rule is that there must be given a set number of trouser opportunities which we call n. Now this is why it actually gets known as the binomial distribution. We all know that the prefix by means two. Well in a binomial problem you must be given two things. A the probability of success which we call p and the second thing you must be given is n a set number of trials or opportunities. Now remember a probability distribution must have two things. First a list of all the outcomes and the second is the probability of each of those outcomes. So since a binomial distribution is a probability distribution we need to have those two two things as well. A list of all possibilities for X and the probabilities of each of those values. Okay. So in a generic example where we have seven trials or seven opportunities for success and the probability of success is given to us as 22% on any given trial. Then X represents how many successes we could have in those seven trials. So what are the possible outcomes for the number of successes we could have in seven trials? Well, we could have zero successes, one success, two success, three, four, five, six, or seven successes. With only seven opportunities, we can't have eight, nine, or 10 successes. That's impossible. And you can't have 2.5 successes. You could either have three or you could have two. So, that's it for the list of our possible values for X, 0 through 7. Nice and simple. Now, what about the probabilities? Well, here is our generic formula to find the probabilities of each of those individual values that X could take on. Now, all of the pieces of this formula could be explained a lot easier if we use an example. So, here it is. Now, David is a member of a frequent flyers club. On any given flight, he is a 22% chance of getting his seat upgraded to first class. in his next seven flights, how many upgrades can he get? Now, let's start off by checking through all of our rules. First, success is clearly defined as getting an upgrade to first class. And the probability of this happening on any given flight is 22%. Now, note not getting an upgrade is failure. That's the complement. 1 minus 0.22 is 0.78. So, we got a 22% chance of successfully getting an upgrade, 78% chance of failing to get an upgrade. Now, we assume since it said that there's a 22% chance of getting an upgrade on any given flight that each flight's chances of an upgrade are independent of all the others. Lastly, a set number of trials or opportunities to get an upgrade is clearly given to us as seven. Now, the list of outcomes is really easy. zero upgrades, one, two, three, four, five, six, or seven upgrades. Now, what about to find the probabilities? Let's start off with getting exactly two upgrades in his next seven flights. Now, one scenario is he gets the upgrade on his first flight. Second flight is a no. Third flight's a no. Fourth flight's a no. Fifth flight is another upgrade. Six and seven flights are both nos. Now yes I'm multiplying a little bit out of order here but does it matter? Order of multiplication doesn't matter, right? I could also use exponents to make it faster. 22 to the 2 times 78 to the 5th. The point is is that I have seven different flights. Two of them have upgrades. Now remember, that's what I don't want. I wanted four or more. But if I find what I don't want, I could do one minus that and I could always get what I do want. So in order for me to find this probability very quickly, all I got to do is a binomial CDF with a three. That's going to give me the probability of 3 2 1 and 0, which is 954. Then I just got to do one minus 954 to get 0.046. So the probability of getting four or more upgrades of the next seven flights is about 4.6%. 046. So again, you have two different ways to do it. Now, some kids could say, well, if the shortcuts are on your calculator, why would I ever need that formula for the binomial distribution and its probabilities? Whether it be for a multiple choice question that's going to show your work, or for an F FRQ, where you kind of have to show your work. Now, like all discrete random variables, we could calculate the mean and standard deviation of the random variable using the formulas that I taught you in the part two video over random variables. But if you have a binomial situation, a binomial scenario, we actually have two amazing shortcuts to find the mean and standard deviation for you a lot faster. What those formulas are going to do for you. Here is the formula for the mean. All you have to do to find the mean of a binomial distribution is take n * p. It's that simple. It's like one of the easiest formulas in the world. To find the standard deviation for a binomial distribution, you have to take the square root of n * p * 1 minus p, which is the complement. Now, in our scenario, the mean is going to be 7 *22, which is 1.54. And the standard deviation is going to be the square root of 7 *22 * 78, which is 1.096. Now, these two formulas for the mean standard deviation of a binomial distribution are given to you on the AP exam. But what matters most is that you know how to explain them. So in our scenario, this would mean that in the long run after many repetitions of this scenario with seven flights and looking for upgrades, we expect to see a average or a mean of 1.54 upgrades out of seven. But of course that number could vary. It could be more, could be less. That's where the standard deviation of 1.096 comes into play. The normal distribution could be used to model continuous random variables that of course follow a normal distribution, which many continuous random variables do. But I guess we should probably quickly remind you what a continuous random variable is. A continuous random variable is a variable that can take on any numerical value within a specified domain. And any interval within that specified domain has a probability associated with it. The one thing unfortunately you cannot do with a continuous random variable is find the probability of an individual specific numerical outcome. Discrete random variables, yes, you could do that, but not with continuous random variables. All we could do is specify an interval, meaning a lower value to an upper value. And within that interval, we could use hopefully the normal distribution if the continuous random variable follows a normal distribution. But if it does, we could use a normal distribution to tell us the probability of a particular value of that continuous random variable falling in that specified interval. So here in this picture, we see a normal distribution and of course hopefully remember a normal distribution is entirely ran by two values. The mean smack dab in the middle and the standard deviation. And the normal distribution tells us that we can go up one, two, three standard deviations down one, two, three standard deviations as well. Now, we could go further up and further down. Technically, the normal distribution goes all the way from negative infinity to infinity. But what the normal distribution tells us is that 99.7% of your variable or of your outcomes falls within three standard deviations. That's negative3 to positive 3. So, in this particular picture, what we're looking at is the area between zcore 1 and zcore 2. and the area in between represents the probability that a outcome of the continuous random variable will fall in that interval. And once again, we could find that probability using a Z table if you know how to use those. They're kind of ancient. Not too many people use them anymore. Or you can also use technology. There's plenty of websites out there, including the TID4 calculator, which has that normal CDF feature that again can find that probability in between two zcores. Now, we've already covered a ton of the normal distribution way back in unit one. I have tons more videos on my YouTube channel about normal model calculations, but let's actually take a look at an example right now so we can quickly remind ourselves how the normal distribution works. Max has been saving money each month for a long time. The amount she contributes to her savings account each month follows a normal distribution with a mean of $55.20 and a standard deviation of $8.15. So, here we're working with a continuous random variable X. How much money does Maxi contribute to her savings account? We know the mean. We know the standard deviation. And we know that it falls a normal distribution, which means that life is going to be really easy for us. Now, the first question here is what is the probability that next month she contributes more than $60. So, the first thing we have to do is figure out on that standard normal model, where does $60 fall? And that's of course by finding the zcore. So we take 60 the value that we're asked about subtract the mean divide by the standard deviation and we get our zcore in this case 0.589 which I have marked with the red line. Then we want to find the probability that a particular outcome how much money she contributes is greater than 60 which is equivalent to a zcore being greater than.589 which I have shaded in red. And again you could use a z table or you could use your t4 calculator to find that probability and we get 27.8%. So there's a 27.8% 8% probability that in any given month she contributes more than $60. Here's a second example following that same problem. This time we're asked what amount of money would indicate the top 5% and bottom 5% of contributions. So once again we have that normal distribution set up and what we have to first do in this case is find the zcores that represent that bottom 5% and that top 5%. Now once again how do you find those zcores? Well, you could use a Z table in backwards, or you could go ahead and use invert norm on your TID4 calculator. And again, we've already learned all that back in unit one. But if you know how to do all that, you could quickly get the Zcore that represents that top 5% and bottom 5%, which is a zcore of either negative 1.645 or positive 1.645. Then what we could do is take those two zcores and plug them into our zcore formula where we already know the mean of $55.20 and the standard deviation of $8.15. So we could back solve for X, multiply by the standard deviation, add the mean over, and we get our two individual values that represent that bottom and top five%. So any value above um $68.61 61 cents would represent the top 5% of contri of contributions and anything below $41.79 would represent the bottom.
